### [EQB-12-05] An important mining company has operated for 4 years in a coastal region. During a trial, the community argues water quality deteriorated and that the company is in a protected area. (a) What document must the company present to prove water was already deteriorated before their activities began, and why is it important? (b) What document proves they are not in a protected area and which institution issues it? (c) Mention 2 environmental impacts and 2 mitigation measures for their EMP.

- **Topic:** Coastal Mining Environmental Audit & Baseline Defense
- **Category:** `Descriptive & Case Study`
- **Marks / Importance:** 10M | CRITICAL (⭐⭐⭐⭐⭐)
- **Exam Appearances:** End Exam 2019 (Section V, Q1), End Exam 2018 (Part I, Q3 (OR))

#### Expected Answer
**Exam Calibrated Answer: 10 Marks (Environmental Audit & Inspection Report)**

**Part (a): Baseline Environmental Status Report (BESR) & Legal Value [3 Marks]:**

- **What is BESR (1M):** The foundational technical document capturing pre-operational environmental conditions (air, groundwater table, water quality, marine flora/fauna, socio-economic baseline) before mining commences.

- **Temporal Variability vs One-Time Snapshot (1M):** Natural systems fluctuate across seasons (monsoon dilution, summer peak salinity, tidal cycles). Multi-season monitoring establishes natural variation ranges, preventing normal seasonal fluctuations from being misattributed to mining operations.

- **Legal Value (1M):** Serves as the sole defense against regulatory prosecution and local litigation under the **Precautionary Principle** and **Polluter Pays Principle**, legally proving whether subsequent contamination originated from the mine or pre-existing natural/background sources.

**Part (b): Mandatory Statutory Approvals for Coastal Operations [3 Marks]:**

- **Environmental Clearance (EC) Letter:** Issued by the Ministry of Environment, Forest and Climate Change (MoEFCC) under the **EIA Notification 2006** (Category A, Schedule 1(a) Mining).

- **Coastal Regulation Zone (CRZ) Clearance Map:** Approved by the State/National Coastal Zone Management Authority (SCZMA/NCZMA) under the **CRZ Notification 2011/2019**, proving the mine and infrastructure are outside prohibited intertidal/No Development Zones (NDZ) of CRZ-I and CRZ-IV.

**Part (c): Diagnosis & Mitigation of Critical Environmental Impacts [4 Marks]:**

- **Acid Mine Drainage (AMD) (2 Marks):**
  
    <li>*Cause:* Oxidation of subsurface iron sulfide minerals (pyrite $\text{FeS}_2$) exposed to atmospheric oxygen and water during open-cast excavation:
    $$2\text{FeS}_2 + 7\text{O}_2 + 2\text{H}_2\text{O} 
ightarrow 2\text{Fe}^{2+} + 4\text{SO}_4^{2-} + 4\text{H}^+$$
    This produces sulfuric acid, which leaches heavy metals into surrounding groundwater.

    - *Mitigation:* Divert run-on water using peripheral drains; line tailings ponds with impermeable geomembranes; treat acidic drainage with active lime ($\text{Ca(OH)}_2$) neutralization tanks and constructed wetland anoxic limestone drains.

  

</li>
- **Saltwater Intrusion (2 Marks):**
  
    <li>*Cause:* Excessive dewatering of deep open pit mines lowers the terrestrial coastal water table below sea level, reversing the hydraulic gradient and pulling saline seawater inland into coastal freshwater aquifers.

    - *Mitigation:* Install bentonite-slurry cutoff walls around pit perimeter; cease indiscriminate dewatering; establish Managed Aquifer Recharge (MAR) injection barrier wells using treated fresh run-off water; deploy piezometer telemetry to monitor the freshwater-saltwater interface continuously.

  

</li>

#### Key Points
- **(a) Baseline Environmental Status Report & Legal Defense:**
<br>• *Document:* The certified **Pre-Project Baseline Environmental Status Report** (specifically the Comprehensive Pre-Operational Baseline Water Quality and Hydrogeological Data) contained within the statutory approved EIA Report.
<br>• *Importance:* Establishes the scientific benchmark of water quality prior to mine construction. As per Class 12 slides, baseline is not a static snapshot but records natural variability and historical trends. Under environmental jurisprudence (Precautionary Principle), the burden of proof rests on the proponent; the approved baseline report is a legally binding document that proves pre-existing deterioration (e.g., natural salinity intrusion or upstream mineral contamination) before year 0, serving as a protective shield against unwarranted liability.

- **(b) Siting / Protected Area Clearance & Issuing Authority:**
<br>• *Documents:* The **Prior Environmental Clearance (EC) Letter** and authenticated **Coastal Regulation Zone (CRZ) Clearance Map / Siting Certificate** with Survey of India geo-coordinates, demonstrating compliance with environmental siting rules (prohibition in CRZ-I intertidal eco-sensitive zones, wetlands, and designated protected areas).
<br>• *Issuing Authority:* The **Ministry of Environment, Forest and Climate Change (MoEFCC)**, Government of India, issued in formal concurrence with the **National / State Coastal Zone Management Authority (NCZMA / SCZMA)**.

- **(c) Two Impacts & Two EMP Mitigation Measures:**
<br>• *Impact 1 (Acid Mine Drainage & Sediment Runoff):* Rainwater leaching through sulfide mineral dumps and open pits generates acidic runoff laden with heavy metals, causing estuarine acidification, sedimentation, and fish mortality.
<br>• *Mitigation 1:* Construct peripheral lined garland drains, multi-chamber settling ponds with lime neutralization dosing to adjust $\text{pH}$ to 7.0–8.5, and geotextile silt curtains at outfalls.
<br>• *Impact 2 (Saltwater Intrusion into Aquifers):* Excessive dewatering of coastal mining pits lowers the groundwater table below sea level, reversing the hydraulic gradient and drawing seawater into regional freshwater drinking wells.
<br>• *Mitigation 2:* Install an engineered impermeable peripheral grout curtain / bentonite slurry cutoff wall around the pit perimeter, coupled with managed aquifer recharge (MAR) injection wells and continuous piezometric automated groundwater monitoring.

#### Detailed Answer
#### Part (a): Document Proving Pre-Existing Water Quality and Its Legal Importance

##### 1. The Required Document

To conclusively prove that regional water quality was already degraded prior to the commencement of mining operations four years ago, the company must present the certified **Pre-Project Baseline Environmental Status Report** (specifically the *Pre-Operational Baseline Water Quality and Hydrogeological Assessment*) that was submitted and formally approved as part of their statutory **Environmental Impact Assessment (EIA) Report** prior to receiving Environmental Clearance.

##### 2. Why It Is Critical (Scientific and Legal Justification)

As established in the foundational principles of EIA (Class 12, Slides 3, 4, and 31):

- **Baseline is Not Simply a “Snapshot”:** In environmental science, baseline data cannot be established from a single sampling round. Describing the baseline situation requires documenting both the *normal seasonal variability* in environmental components (e.g., monsoonal dilution vs. pre-monsoon concentration) and *historical temporal trends*. The pre-project baseline report contains multi-season hydrochemical data (salinity, total dissolved solids $\text{TDS}$, sulfates, heavy metals, $\text{pH}$) collected before any mining pit was excavated.

- **Legal Status of EIA as a Binding Document:** As stated in Slide 31, *“EIA is a legal document and any attempt to provide wrong facts or downplaying of relevant information is an offence.”* Conversely, when rigorously prepared by certified professionals, *“the EIA study is a protective weapon against vested interest groups and will defend scientifically and legally the right for existence of an environment-friendly project.”*

- **Reversal of the Burden of Proof:** Under environmental jurisprudence (the **Precautionary Principle** affirmed by environmental courts and tribunals), the legal burden of proof (*onus probandi*) rests entirely on the industrial proponent. Without authenticated pre-project baseline data, the court will legally presume that all observed water deterioration is attributable to the mining operation. The baseline report provides legally admissible empirical evidence demonstrating that high background salinity or mineral leaching existed prior to project inception due to natural coastal geology, tidal dynamics, or upstream third-party discharges.

<hr class="exam-divider">

#### Part (b): Document Proving Non-Encroachment on Protected Areas and Issuing Institution

##### 1. Required Documents

To establish conclusively that the mining lease does not encroach upon any protected or prohibited ecological zone, the company must present two official statutory clearances:

- **Prior Environmental Clearance (EC) Letter:** The formal clearance granted under Schedule-I (Item 20: *Mining projects of major minerals with leases $> 5\text{ hectares}$*).

- **Coastal Regulation Zone (CRZ) Clearance & Authenticated Demarcation Siting Map:** A georeferenced cadastral boundary map prepared by an authorized Central agency (such as the National Centre for Sustainable Coastal Management - NCSCM or the Survey of India). This document officially establishes that the mining boundaries lie strictly outside **CRZ-I** (ecologically sensitive intertidal zones, mangroves, marine turtle nesting beaches) and outside prohibited statutory buffer zones (such as the mandatory $7\text{ km}$ buffer around wetlands and national parks prescribed in Environmental Siting Rules; Class 12, Slides 29–30).

##### 2. Issuing Institution

In accordance with the statutory framework (Class 12, Slide 23):

- The **Ministry of Environment, Forest and Climate Change (MoEFCC)**, Government of India, is the supreme central authority that grants Environmental Clearance for major mineral mining projects.

- For coastal areas, clearances are issued in formal concurrence with the **National Coastal Zone Management Authority (NCZMA)** and the respective **State Coastal Zone Management Authority (SCZMA)** under the Coastal Regulation Zone Notification issued pursuant to the Environment (Protection) Act, 1986.

<hr class="exam-divider">

#### Part (c): Two Environmental Impacts and Mitigation Measures for the EMP

##### Impact 1: Acid Mine Drainage (AMD) and Heavy Metal Sediment Runoff

**Description:** Coastal surface mining exposes sub-surface sulfide minerals (such as pyrite, $\text{FeS}_2$) to atmospheric oxygen and heavy monsoon precipitation. The resulting biochemical oxidation generates highly acidic drainage ($2\text{FeS}_2 + 7\text{O}_2 + 2\text{H}_2\text{O} \rightarrow 2\text{Fe}^{2+} + 4\text{SO}_4^{2-} + 4\text{H}^+$) that mobilizes toxic heavy metals ($\text{Fe, Cu, Zn, Mn}$) into nearby estuaries, destroying marine benthic fauna, decimating fisheries, and smothering mangrove pneumatophores.

**EMP Mitigation Measures:**

- **Runoff Interception & Sedimentation:** Excavate concrete-lined peripheral garland drains around the entire perimeter of the mine pits and overburden dumps to divert clean storm runoff away from active workings. Route all mine drainage into a multi-stage settling basin equipped with silt retention baffles.

- **Active Neutralization & Polishing:** Install an automated chemical neutralization dosing plant (utilizing hydrated lime, $\text{Ca(OH)}_2$) to raise effluent $\text{pH}$ to $7.5–8.5$, precipitating heavy metals as insoluble metal hydroxides. Deploy marine geotextile silt curtains across discharge channels to trap colloidal suspended sediment before water enters coastal water bodies.

##### Impact 2: Saltwater Intrusion into Coastal Aquifers Due to Pit Dewatering

**Description:** To maintain dry mining conditions below the coastal water table, extensive deep dewatering pumping is conducted. Continuous dewatering creates a regional cone of depression that depresses the inland hydraulic head below sea level. This reverses the natural seaward groundwater gradient, inducing rapid seawater intrusion into surrounding freshwater aquifers, ruining domestic wells and salinizing coastal agricultural soils.

**EMP Mitigation Measures:**

- **Physical Hydrogeological Barrier:** Install an engineered peripheral impermeable slurry cutoff wall (bentonite-cement grout curtain) around the seaward perimeter of the deep mining pit to physically block hydraulic communication between the ocean and the dewatered inland excavation.

- **Managed Aquifer Recharge (MAR) & Piezometric Surveillance:** Establish an artificial recharge injection well barrier along the property boundary using harvested treated stormwater to maintain positive freshwater hydrostatic pressure against the sea. Install a real-time automated piezometric telemetry network with electrical conductivity sensors to continuously monitor salinity profiles and groundwater levels (Class 12, Slide 4).

*Slide Reference: Class 12, Slides 3, 4, 18, 20, 22, 23, 27–31.*

---

### [EQB-12-06] List any two incorrect/incomplete information furnished in the EIA report of Great Nicobar case.

- **Topic:** EIA Report Quality, Misrepresentation & Flaws (Great Nicobar Case)
- **Category:** `Short Answer & Definitions`
- **Marks / Importance:** 2M | HIGH (⭐⭐⭐⭐)
- **Exam Appearances:** End Exam 2023 (Section II, Q11)

#### Expected Answer
**Exam Calibrated Answer: 2 Marks (1 Mark each)**

**Two Deficiencies in the Great Nicobar Island EIA Report:**

- **Underreporting Endangered Marine Habitats (1 Mark):** The EIA report downplayed Galathea Bay as an uninhabited shoreline, ignoring that it is one of the world's most critical nesting sites for the endangered **Giant Leatherback Sea Turtle** (*Dermochelys coriacea*) and Nicobar Megapode bird.

- **Violating Tribal Rights & Geological Hazards (1 Mark):** Overlooked the de-notification of $\sim 84\text{ km}^2$ of tribal reserve land belonging to indigenous **Shompen and Nicobarese** tribes, while completely failing to address severe tectonic and tsunami risks in high-hazard **Seismic Zone V**.

#### Key Points
- **Statutory Mandate on Report Integrity:** As emphasized in Class 12 Slide 31, *"EIA is a legal document and any attempt to provide wrong facts or down playing of relevant information is an offence."* Slide 32 specifically directs study of the Great Nicobar mega-project EIA controversies.

- **Flaw 1 (Underreporting / Omission of Sea Turtle Nesting Sanctuaries):** The EIA report downplayed the ecological significance of Galathea Bay, claiming low nesting density, whereas Galathea Bay is globally acknowledged as one of the world's most critical nesting sanctuaries for the vulnerable **Giant Leatherback Sea Turtle** (*Dermochelys coriacea*) and the endemic Nicobar Megapode bird.

- **Flaw 2 (Ignoring / Glossing Over Indigenous Tribal Rights):** The EIA failed to adequately account for irreversible impacts on Particularly Vulnerable Tribal Groups (PVTGs)—specifically the semi-nomadic **Shompen** and the coastal **Nicobarese**—omitting the full implications of de-notifying nearly $84\text{ km}^2$ of pristine Tribal Reserve forest.

- **Flaw 3 (Severe Under-Assessment of Extreme Seismic & Tsunami Hazards):** Great Nicobar is situated in **Seismic Zone V** along the active Sunda subduction megathrust (the epicentral zone of the 2004 earthquake that caused 3–4 meters of island land subsidence); the EIA failed to incorporate adequate seismic risk analysis and tectonic stability assessments for the deep-sea transshipment port.

- **Flaw 4 (Rushed Single-Season Baseline Data):** Violated the fundamental EIA principle that baseline data requires temporal variability and trends (Slide 4), utilizing rapid, single-season surveys during monsoonal conditions that severely under-recorded rich coral reef and marine biodiversity.

#### Detailed Answer
#### 1. Legal Framework and Background Context

As explicitly laid out in the lecture conclusions (Class 12, Slide 31):

- **EIA is a Statutory Legal Document:** *“EIA is a legal document and any attempt to provide wrong facts or downplaying of relevant information is an offence.”* Under Section 15 of the Environment (Protection) Act, 1986, furnishing false scientific data or deliberately concealing environmental risks carries strict civil and criminal liabilities.

- **The Consultant Economy Trap:** Slide 31 further warns that *“many project proponents consider investment for an EIA study as a burden and settle for economical rates and ultimately end up with inordinate delays for want of factual data by the authorities.”*

- Class 12, Slide 32 specifically directs students to examine the controversial Environmental Clearance granted to the ₹72,000-crore **Great Nicobar Island Mega-Transshipment Port, International Airport, Power Plant, and Township Project**.

#### 2. Major Inaccurate and Incomplete Information Furnished in the EIA

Among the critical flaws and misrepresented data exposed in the Great Nicobar EIA report, the primary issues include:

##### 1. Underreporting of Prime Nesting Grounds for the Giant Leatherback Turtle

The EIA report significantly downplayed the ecological sensitivity of Galathea Bay—the exact site selected for the construction of the deep-sea international container transshipment terminal. The consultant’s report claimed that the beach had low ecological sensitivity and that nesting activities could easily be translocated. In reality, Galathea Bay is recognized globally by marine biologists and the Indian Wildlife Institute as one of the premier nesting sanctuaries on Earth for the globally vulnerable **Giant Leatherback Sea Turtle (*Dermochelys coriacea*)**, as well as the endangered endemic Nicobar Megapode (*Megapodius nicobariensis*). Constructing heavy port breakwaters, dredging, and installing industrial high-intensity lighting causes total destruction of this irreplaceable nesting habitat.

##### 2. Inadequate Assessment of Indigenous Tribal Rights and Forest De-Notification

The EIA study furnished severely incomplete information regarding the project’s impact on indigenous populations. Great Nicobar is home to two isolated indigenous communities: the coastal Nicobarese and the semi-nomadic, hunter-gatherer **Shompen tribe** (classified as a *Particularly Vulnerable Tribal Group - PVTG*, numbering only ~200–300 individuals). The EIA glossed over the irreversible socio-cultural disruption and failed to conduct genuine free, prior, and informed consultations with the Shompen. Crucially, the report downplayed the massive de-notification of approximately **$84\text{ km}^2$ of declared Tribal Reserve forest**, violating the letter and spirit of constitutional and statutory tribal safeguards.

##### 3. Severe Underestimation of Seismic and Tsunami Hazards

Great Nicobar Island lies directly along the seismically volatile Sunda Trench in **Seismic Zone V** (the highest earthquake hazard category). During the 9.1-magnitude Great Sumatra-Andaman earthquake on 26th December 2004, the island experienced massive crustal subsidence, with the shoreline dropping permanently by $3\text{ to }4\text{ meters}$ and the lighthouse submerged. Despite this extreme tectonic history, the EIA report treated the coastal geology as structurally stable, omitting detailed hydrodynamic tsunami simulations, liquefaction analysis, and tectonic subsidence assessments for the proposed breakwaters and airport runway.

##### 4. Violation of Baseline Temporal Monitoring Protocols

Contrary to the core EIA principle taught in Class 12, Slide 4 (which dictates that *“describing the baseline situation requires describing both the normal variability in environmental components & current trends”*), the EIA consultants conducted a rapid, single-season baseline assessment during an unrepresentative period. This rushed sampling missed critical seasonal fish migrations, seasonal marine benthic fauna, and extensive fringing coral reef ecosystems that would be destroyed by dredging.

*Slide Reference: Class 12, Slides 15, 20, 31, 32.*

### [EQB-12-07] Explain the four core stages of Environmental Impact Assessment (EIA) according to the Indian EIA Notification 2006.

- **Topic:** Four Core Stages of Indian EIA Process (2006 Notification)
- **Category:** `Descriptive & Case Study`
- **Marks / Importance:** 4M | CRITICAL (⭐⭐⭐⭐⭐)
- **Exam Appearances:** End Exam 2024 (Section V), End Exam 2023 (Section II, Q9)

#### Expected Answer
**Exam Calibrated Answer: 4 Marks (1 Mark per stage)**

**Four Core Stages of Indian EIA 2006 Notification (Schedule 1):**

- **Stage 1: Screening (1 Mark):** Applicable strictly to Category 'B' projects. The State Expert Appraisal Committee (SEAC) scrutinizes the application to classify the project into **Category B1** (mandatory full EIA and public hearing) or **Category B2** (exempt from EIA study and public consultation). Category 'A' projects automatically require full EIA.

- **Stage 2: Scoping (1 Mark):** The Expert Appraisal Committee (EAC or SEAC) determines comprehensive, site-specific **Terms of Reference (ToR)** defining all environmental aspects, study boundaries (typically 10 km radius), and seasonal baseline parameters to be investigated.

- **Stage 3: Public Consultation (1 Mark):** Involves two statutory components: (a) a formal **Public Hearing** at the project site conducted by the SPCB and chaired by the District Magistrate/Collector (mandatory video recording), and (b) obtaining written responses/objections from concerned stakeholders with 30-day notice.

- **Stage 4: Appraisal (1 Mark):** The EAC/SEAC undertakes detailed technical scrutiny of the final EIA report, outcome of public consultations, and EMP, and makes formal categorical recommendations to the regulatory authority (MoEFCC or SEIAA) to either **Grant** or **Reject** Environmental Clearance.

#### Key Points
- **Regulatory Foundation:** Promulgated on 14th September 2006 under the Environment (Protection) Act, 1986, superseding the 1994 notification. It established a decentralized clearance process, categorizing projects into Category A (Central MoEFCC / EAC) and Category B (State SEIAA / SEAC).

- **Stage 1: Screening:** Applied strictly to Category B projects to determine whether a project requires a full EIA study (**Category B1**) or is appraised directly based on Form 1/1A without an EIA study (**Category B2**). Category A projects bypass screening and require mandatory full EIA.

- **Stage 2: Scoping:** Formulates detailed, comprehensive **Terms of Reference (ToR)** by the Expert Appraisal Committee (EAC / SEAC) delineating all environmental concerns, baseline study boundaries (e.g., 10 km radius), multi-season sampling parameters, and impact modeling guidelines.

- **Stage 3: Public Consultation:** Structured process ensuring transparency and local stakeholder engagement. Contains two mandatory limbs: (a) *Public Hearing* at the project site conducted by the State Pollution Control Board (SPCB) and presided by the District Collector/Magistrate (videographed); and (b) *Written Stakeholder Representations*. Proponent must address all concerns in the Final EIA/EMP.

- **Stage 4: Appraisal:** Multidisciplinary scrutiny by the EAC/SEAC of the Final EIA report, public hearing proceedings, and EMP to formulate categorical recommendations to MoEFCC/SEIAA for the grant of Prior Environmental Clearance (with binding conditions) or rejection.

#### Detailed Answer
#### 1. Regulatory Framework and Decentralization

The **EIA Notification of 14th September 2006**, issued by the Ministry of Environment and Forests (MoEF, now MoEFCC) under the **Environment (Protection) Act, 1986**, fundamentally restructured the environmental clearance process in India. It replaced the centralized single-tier system of the 1994 notification with a decentralized two-tier architectural framework:

- **Category A Projects:** Large-scale, high-risk, or nationally significant projects (e.g., ports, nuclear energy, petroleum refineries, large thermal power plants) appraised at the Central level by the **Expert Appraisal Committee (EAC)** and cleared by the Union Ministry of Environment, Forest and Climate Change (MoEFCC).

- **Category B Projects:** Medium- and smaller-scale industrial and infrastructure activities appraised at the State level by the **State Expert Appraisal Committee (SEAC)** and cleared by the **State Environment Impact Assessment Authority (SEIAA)**.

Under the 2006 Notification, the statutory Environmental Clearance process is structured into **four sequential, interdependent core stages**:

| **1. Screening** 
| Determines whether a project requires a full EIA study 
| State Expert Appraisal Committee (SEAC) 
| Form 1 / Form 1A scrutiny (B1 vs. B2 classification) 

| **2. Scoping** 
| Determines comprehensive Terms of Reference (ToR) for the study 
| EAC (Category A) / SEAC (Category B1) 
| Formal Terms of Reference (ToR) 

| **3. Public Consultation** 
| Ascertains concerns of locally affected persons and stakeholders 
| State Pollution Control Board (SPCB) & Dist. Magistrate 
| Public Hearing proceedings & written representations 

| **4. Appraisal** 
| Rigorous multidisciplinary evaluation to recommend clearance or rejection 
| Expert Appraisal Committee (EAC / SEAC) 
| Final EIA Report, EMP, and formal recommendations 

<hr class="exam-divider">

#### 2. Detailed Examination of the Four Core Stages

##### Stage 1: Screening

Screening is the initial filtering mechanism. Crucially, under the 2006 Notification, **screening is conducted only for Category B projects**. All Category A projects mandatorily require a full EIA study and bypass screening entirely.

The project proponent submits statutory application **Form 1** (and Form 1A for construction projects). The State Expert Appraisal Committee (SEAC) scrutinizes the spatial scale, raw materials, resource consumption, and proximity to sensitive ecosystems to classify the project into one of two sub-categories:

- **Category B1:** Projects that possess substantial potential to alter the environment. These projects **mandatorily require a full EIA study**, comprehensive baseline monitoring, and public consultation before appraisal.

- **Category B2:** Projects characterized by smaller capacities or low environmental footprints (e.g., small non-coal mining leases, minor manufacturing units). Category B2 projects are **completely exempted from conducting a full EIA study and public hearing**, and are appraised directly based on their Form 1 and standard Environmental Management Plan.

##### Stage 2: Scoping

Scoping is the critical stage where the regulatory committee defines the exact scientific and geographic boundaries of the study. It ensures that the EIA focuses strictly on significant potential impacts without wasting time and financial resources on trivial matters (as emphasized in Class 12, Slide 5).

- For all Category A and Category B1 projects, the EAC or SEAC examines the proposed site, project technology, and potential hazards to formulate comprehensive, site-specific **Terms of Reference (ToR)**.

- The ToR explicitly dictates: the study area radius (typically $10\text{ km}$ buffer); baseline environmental parameters to be monitored across seasons (air, water quality, noise, soils, flora, fauna, and health); mathematical dispersion modeling methods to be used; alternative sites and technologies to be evaluated; and specific risk assessment protocols.

- The project proponent must strictly adhere to this ToR when collecting baseline field data and preparing the Draft EIA Report.

##### Stage 3: Public Consultation

Public Consultation is the statutory mechanism that guarantees transparency, accountability, and participatory democracy in environmental decision-making. As highlighted in Class 12, Slide 20, *“predicting impacts is facilitated by broad-based public consultation; judging significance is very difficult without it.”*

Public Consultation consists of two mandatory components:

- **Public Hearing:** Conducted at or in close proximity to the project site to ascertain the views, objections, and concerns of locally affected communities. It is organized by the respective State Pollution Control Board (SPCB) and presided over by the District Collector, District Magistrate, or an authorized administrative officer. The entire proceeding is videographed, and verbatim minutes (in both English and local language) are signed and forwarded to the regulatory committee.

- **Obtaining Written Stakeholder Representations:** Inviting written comments, objections, and scientific suggestions from other concerned citizens, civil society groups, and environmental researchers who have a plausible stake in the environmental aspects of the project.

Following public consultation, the project proponent is legally mandated to address all material environmental concerns raised by the public and append a dedicated chapter detailing how these concerns have been incorporated into the **Final Environmental Management Plan (EMP)**.

##### Stage 4: Appraisal

Appraisal represents the final, formal scientific and administrative evaluation. The Expert Appraisal Committee (EAC at MoEFCC) or State Expert Appraisal Committee (SEAC) undertakes detailed multi-disciplinary scrutiny of:

- The Final Environmental Impact Assessment (EIA) Report;

- The unedited minutes and videography of the Public Hearing;

- The commitments made in the Environmental Management Plan (EMP);

- Compliance with each parameter stipulated in the original Terms of Reference (ToR).

As detailed in Class 12, Slide 24, the appraisal committee draws upon diverse specialists (ecosystem management, pollution control, flora/fauna conservation, rehabilitation, environmental health, and civil society). The committee invites the project proponent and their accredited environmental consultants for a comprehensive technical defense.

Upon completion of appraisal, the EAC or SEAC formulates categorical recommendations to the regulatory authority (MoEFCC or SEIAA):

- **Grant of Prior Environmental Clearance:** Recommending clearance along with legally binding, enforceable conditions (general and specific safeguards, zero liquid discharge mandates, continuous online emission monitoring systems, green belt development covenants); or

- **Rejection of Environmental Clearance:** Recommending outright rejection with recorded, scientifically grounded reasons (e.g., unacceptable siting near pristine wetlands, excessive groundwater depletion, or destruction of critical biodiversity).

The regulatory authority (MoEFCC or SEIAA) delivers the final decision, which is published on the public portal for environmental transparency.

*Slide Reference: Class 12, Slides 7, 8, 11, 12, 17–20, 23–25.*

## Class 13: Grassroots Environmental Movements in India

### [EQB-13-01] Slogan of "Chipko movement".

- **Topic:** Chipko Movement Slogan & Ecological Philosophy
- **Category:** `Short Answer & Definitions`
- **Marks / Importance:** 1M | CRITICAL (⭐⭐⭐⭐⭐)
- **Exam Appearances:** End Exam 2023 (Section I, Q1(h))

#### Expected Answer
**Exam Calibrated Answer: 1 Mark**

**Primary Slogans of Chipko Movement:**

- **Core Philosophical Slogan:** *"Ecology is permanent economy"* (coined by **Sunderlal Bahuguna**).

- **Grassroots Folk Rhyme:** *"What do the forests bear? Soil, water and pure air. Soil, water and pure air are the basis of staying alive."* (Chamoli women / Gaura Devi, 1973).

*(Writing either slogan earns full 1 Mark).*

#### Key Points
- **Core Ecological Slogan:** *'Ecology is permanent economy'* (coined and popularized internationally by environmentalist **Sunderlal Bahuguna**).

- **Grassroots Verse Slogan (Hindi & English):**
<br>• *Hindi:* *"क्या हैं जंगल के उपकार? मिट्टी, पानी और बयार। मिट्टी, पानी और बयार, ज़िंदा रहने के आधार।"*
<br>• *English Translation:* *"What do the forests bear? Soil, water and pure air. Soil, water and pure air are the basis of staying alive."*

- **Focal Defiance Quotes:**
<br>• **Chandi Prasad Bhatt:** *"Let them know we will not allow the felling of a single tree. When their men raise their axes, we will embrace the trees to protect them."*
<br>• **Gaura Devi:** *"The forest nurtures us like a mother; you will only be able to use your axes on it but you have to use them first on us."*

#### Detailed Answer
**The Core Ecological Slogan:** The most internationally acclaimed motto of the Chipko Movement is **'Ecology is permanent economy'**, coined by Gandhian philosopher and environmentalist **Sunderlal Bahuguna**. This concise phrase marked a historic paradigm shift in environmental economics. For decades, colonial forestry and post-independence commercial policies viewed Himalayan forests merely as short-term extractive revenue generators—quantified by board-feet of timber, trainloads of pulpwood, and resin extracted for chemical industries. Bahuguna stood this logic on its head, proving that the real, permanent economic value of the forest lies in its ecological life-support functions: anchoring topsoil, regulating freshwater hydrology, preventing destructive downstream floods, and generating clean air.

#### The Folk Rhyme Born in Village Rallies

Alongside Bahuguna's philosophical slogan, rural hill communities chanted an iconic call-and-response folk verse during village assemblies and protest marches in Uttarakhand:

<p style="text-align:center; font-weight:600; font-size:1.05rem; margin:0.3rem 0;">"क्या हैं जंगल के उपकार? मिट्टी, पानी और बयार।<br>मिट्टी, पानी और बयार, ज़िंदा रहने के आधार।"</p>
<p style="text-align:center; font-style:italic; margin:0.3rem 0;">("What do the forests bear? Soil, water and pure air.<br>Soil, water and pure air are the basis of staying alive.")</p>

This verse directly countered commercial timber contractors who argued that the forest bears resin, timber, and money, asserting instead that life itself depends on unfragmented watersheds.

#### Historical Roots & The Spark at Gopeshwar (1973)

The movement ignited over deep-seated economic and resource inequalities. In 1973, local mountain residents of Gopeshwar (Chamoli district) were formally denied permission by the state forest department to fell a modest quota of ash trees needed to craft basic wooden yokes and agricultural tools. Simultaneously, the forest department granted commercial logging permits for the exact same forest tract to Symonds, a large sporting goods manufacturer based in Allahabad. In response, social reformer **Chandi Prasad Bhatt** and the *Dasoli Gram Swarajya Sangh* (DGSS) rallied villagers to protect their indigenous forest resources, demanding small, locally managed forest-based cooperatives to create village employment and curb youth out-migration.

#### Gaura Devi and the Historic Stand at Reni (1974)

The confrontation reached its defining moment in March 1974 at Reni village. Taking advantage of a state-orchestrated ploy that drew village men away to Chamoli town to collect compensation dues, logging contractors and forest officials entered the forest with axes. **Gaura Devi**, an elderly village woman and leader of the local *Mahila Mangal Dal*, mobilized 27 village women. Stepping directly into the path of the armed lumberjacks, she courageously declared: *"The forest nurtures us like a mother; you will only be able to use your axes on it but you have to use them first on us."* The women formed a human cordon around the trees, forcing the loggers to retreat empty-handed.

#### National Mobilization and Legal Victory

Supported by **Sarla Behn** (disciple of Mahatma Gandhi from Lakshmi Ashram, Kausani), organized women's groups, and the **Uttarakhand Sangharsh Vahini** (USV), activists staged widespread rallies, disrupted forest commercial auctions, and elevated regional tree-hugging into a national crusade. Bahuguna took this message on foot across 5,000 km of Himalayan mountain villages, eventually presenting the crisis to Prime Minister Indira Gandhi. This resulted in an unprecedented 15-year statutory moratorium on commercial tree felling above 1,000 meters elevation in the Uttarakhand Himalayas in 1980.

*Slide Reference: Class 13, Slides 4, 5, 6.*

### [EQB-13-02] ______ (person) led both Chipko and Tehri dam movement.

- **Topic:** Sunderlal Bahuguna Leadership (Chipko & Tehri Dam)
- **Category:** `MCQ & Objective`
- **Marks / Importance:** 1M | HIGH (⭐⭐⭐⭐)
- **Exam Appearances:** End Exam 2022 (Section III, Q7)

#### Expected Answer
**Exam Calibrated Answer: 1 Mark**

**Answer:** **Sunderlal Bahuguna**

**Exam Context (1 Mark):** Prominent Gandhian environmentalist who coined the slogan *"Ecology is permanent economy"*, undertook foot-marches during the 1970s Chipko movement, and later led the anti-Tehri Dam movement with prolonged hunger strikes opposing dam construction in the fragile Himalayan seismic belt.

#### Key Points
- **Correct Answer:** **Sunderlal Bahuguna** (1927–2021).

- **Leadership in Chipko Movement (Slides 4, 6):** Enlightened the entire nation and global community about the ecological importance of Himalayan forests, popularized the slogan *'Ecology is permanent economy'*, undertook a 5,000 km trans-Himalayan padayatra, and secured a 15-year commercial green felling ban from Prime Minister Indira Gandhi in 1980. Awarded the Padma Vibhushan.

- **Leadership in Tehri Dam Agitation (Slides 11, 12):** Lived in a humble hut on the banks of the Bhagirathi River for decades; spearheaded prolonged hunger strikes (including a 45-day fast in 1995 and a 74-day fast in 1996) demanding independent reviews of seismic hazards, submergence risks, and ecological degradation in the Central Himalayan seismic gap.

#### Detailed Answer
**The Answer:** **Sunderlal Bahuguna** is the visionary Gandhian environmentalist who led both the **Chipko Movement** and the **Tehri Dam Movement**.

#### Role in the Chipko Movement (1970s–1980s)

While grassroots leaders like Chandi Prasad Bhatt and Gaura Devi spearheaded the direct physical action of hugging trees in Chamoli, Sunderlal Bahuguna served as the movement's intellectual soul and international ambassador. Mentored in non-violent rural resistance by **Sarla Behn** at Lakshmi Ashram, Bahuguna effectively mobilized village communities, youth, and women's cooperatives across Garhwal and Kumaon. Between 1981 and 1983, Bahuguna undertook an arduous **5,000-kilometer trans-Himalayan foot march (padayatra)** from Kashmir to Kohima, crossing remote mountain villages to document ecological devastation caused by deforestation, road construction, and monoculture plantations.

His philosophical declaration—*'Ecology is permanent economy'*—reframed forest protection not as anti-development obstructionism, but as essential survival infrastructure. His persistent appeals directly persuaded Prime Minister Indira Gandhi to institute a landmark 15-year statutory ban on green tree felling in Himalayan forests in 1980. For his monumental contributions to environmental protection, Bahuguna was honored with the Padma Vibhushan.

#### Role in the Anti-Tehri Dam Agitation (1980s–2000s)

Following his victories in Chipko, Bahuguna turned his focus to opposing the construction of the colossal 260.5-meter-high **Tehri Dam** on the Bhagirathi River in Tehri Garhwal. Grounding his arguments in geology, seismology, and cultural ecology, he warned that erecting a massive earth-and-rockfill dam in the tectonically active **Central Himalayan seismic gap (Seismic Zone V)** represented an unacceptable existential risk to downstream holy cities and millions of citizens along the Indo-Gangetic plains.

Bahuguna gave up worldly comforts and established a simple riverside hermitage (kutir) directly within the submergence zone on the banks of the Bhagirathi. From there, he waged a relentless non-violent satyagraha, undertaking historic extended fasts—including a 45-day hunger strike in 1995 and a grueling 74-day fast in 1996. Through these actions, he compelled the central government to establish independent expert review committees to re-examine the dam's seismic design safety, river ecology, and displacement rehabilitation plans, remaining at the site until rising reservoir waters submerged his hut in 2004.

*Slide Reference: Class 13, Slides 4, 5, 6, 11, 12.*

### [EQB-13-03] What are the negative impacts of Tehri Dam?

- **Topic:** Negative Impacts of Tehri Dam
- **Category:** `Short Answer & Definitions`
- **Marks / Importance:** 2M – 3M | CRITICAL (⭐⭐⭐⭐⭐)
- **Exam Appearances:** End Exam 2024 (Section V, Q4), End Exam 2023 (Section III, Q4)

#### Expected Answer
**Exam Calibrated Answer: 2M – 3M**

**[For 2 Marks — Write any 2 distinct points (1M each)]:**

- **High Seismic & Dam-Break Hazard (1 Mark):** Built in high-risk Seismic Zone V near active Himalayan fault lines (Main Central Thrust); a major earthquake ($\ge 8.0$) poses catastrophic failure risks, threatening downstream cities (Rishikesh, Haridwar).

- **Mass Involuntary Displacement (1 Mark):** Inundated the historic town of Old Tehri and over 100 villages, permanently displacing over 100,000 people and destroying cultural heritage.

**[If asked for 3 Marks, add 3rd point]:**

<ol start="3">
- **Downstream Ecological Destruction & Siltation (1 Mark):** Diverting the Bhagirathi river dries up downstream channels, traps fertile silt in the reservoir dead storage, and destroys breeding grounds of endangered fish like the **Golden Mahseer**.

</ol>

#### Key Points
- **Extreme Seismic Vulnerability (Central Himalayan Gap):** Constructed in Seismic Zone V near active tectonic fault lines (Main Himalayan Thrust). An earthquake of magnitude $\ge 8.0$ could trigger dam failure, unleashing a catastrophic flood surge that would drown downstream pilgrimage cities (Rishikesh, Haridwar) in hours.

- **Mass Involuntary Displacement & Cultural Submergence:** Inundated the historic capital town of Old Tehri and over 100 surrounding villages, permanently displacing over 100,000 residents and severing deep ancestral, social, and agrarian ties.

- **Reservoir-Induced Seismicity (RIS) & Rim Landslides:** The enormous weight of 4 billion cubic meters of impounded water exerts severe hydraulic pore pressure on fractured mountain rock, triggering slope collapses and localized tremors.

- **Destruction of Riparian Ecology & Bhagirathi Flow:** River diversion into tunnels starves the natural riverbed of ecological baseflow, blocks sediment and nutrient transport, and destroys the migration and breeding grounds of endemic cold-water fish like the Golden Mahseer.

- **Accelerated Sedimentation & Reduced Operating Life:** High erosion from fragile, deforested Himalayan catchments causes heavy silt buildup behind coffer and main dam walls, choking reservoir capacity far faster than planned engineering lifespans.

#### Detailed Answer
**Overview:** The **Tehri Dam** on the Bhagirathi River in Uttarakhand is one of the world's tallest earth-and-rockfill dams (height: 260.5 meters). While engineered to generate 1,000 MW of peaking hydroelectric power and supply drinking water to Delhi and Uttar Pradesh, its construction sparked intense controversy due to catastrophic geological hazards, massive socio-cultural displacement, and severe ecological degradation.

#### 1. Catastrophic Seismic Hazard (Central Himalayan Seismic Gap)

The paramount concern raised by geologists, seismologists, and environmentalists is the dam's hazardous tectonic location. Tehri Dam sits in **Seismic Zone V** within the Central Himalayan seismic gap—a region where tectonic stress between the underthrusting Indian tectonic plate and the Eurasian plate has been accumulating for centuries without a major strain release. The dam is situated close to active faults, including the Maroda tear fault and the Main Central Thrust (MCT). Seismologists warned that an earthquake exceeding magnitude 8.0 to 8.5 could induce soil liquefaction, structural overtopping, or dam breach. A catastrophic dam rupture would unleash a tidal wave of 4 billion cubic meters of water, submerging the holy downstream pilgrimage cities of Rishikesh within 60 minutes and Haridwar shortly thereafter, putting millions of lives at risk.

#### 2. Submergence of Historic Tehri and Involuntary Displacement

The filling of the 42-square-kilometer reservoir permanently drowned **Old Tehri**, a centuries-old historic capital town, cultural center, and educational hub of the Garhwal region. Along with the town, more than 100 agricultural villages and fertile valley river terraces were inundated. Over **100,000 people** were involuntarily uprooted. Resettlement processes were plagued by administrative delays, incomplete land allocations, fragmented community structures, and inadequate livelihood transitions for hill farmers relocated to unfamiliar plains.

#### 3. Reservoir-Induced Seismicity (RIS) and Slope Instability

Impounding vast volumes of water creates enormous hydrostatic overburden pressure. As water seeps into underlying joints and faults, pore water pressure increases, lubricating fault planes and triggering **Reservoir-Induced Seismicity (RIS)**. Furthermore, the daily fluctuation of reservoir levels during peak hydropower generation destabilizes the steep, fractured shale slopes surrounding the lake rim, triggering massive landslides, road washouts, and building fissures in lakeside villages.

#### 4. Ecological Destruction of the Bhagirathi Aquatic Ecosystem

Constructing coffer dams and diverting the Bhagirathi River through concrete headrace tunnels severed river continuity. The natural flow regime was transformed into a stagnant, thermally stratified lacustrine body. This blockage traps nutrient-rich silt, disrupts natural river self-purification, and permanently blocks upstream migration routes for native cold-water fish species, most notably the endangered **Golden Mahseer** (*Tor putitora*) and snow trout, leading to severe population crashes.

#### 5. Accelerated Siltation and Compromised Lifespan

The young, highly sheared geological formations of the upper Bhagirathi catchment suffer from natural slope instability, compounded by deforestation and glacial melt. Heavy seasonal monsoon rains wash millions of tonnes of bedload sediment into the reservoir. This accelerated siltation fills dead storage space at rates dramatically higher than original project forecasts, threatening to shorten the dam's economic lifespan and abrade power turbine blades.

*Slide Reference: Class 13, Slides 11, 12.*

---

### [EQB-13-04] What are the environmental issues associated with Kali River of Western ghats?

- **Topic:** Kali River Environmental Issues & Kali Bachao Andolan
- **Category:** `Short Answer & Definitions`
- **Marks / Importance:** 3M | HIGH (⭐⭐⭐⭐)
- **Exam Appearances:** End Exam 2023 (Section III, Q3)

#### Expected Answer
**Exam Calibrated Answer: 3 Marks**

**Three Major Environmental Issues of Kali River (Western Ghats, Karnataka) [1 Mark per point]:**

- **Hydrological Fragmentation (Dams) (1 Mark):** Construction of six major dams (Supa, Bommanahalli, Kodasalli, Kadra, Kaneri, Tattihalla) impounded over $320\text{ km}^2$ of river valley, fragmenting pristine tropical rainforests and altering downstream flows.

- **Industrial Toxic Pollution (Paper Mill) (1 Mark):** West Coast Paper Mills at Dandeli discharges untreated effluents (lignin, chlorinated organics, bleaching toxins), severely depleting dissolved oxygen and killing aquatic biodiversity.

- **Catchment Soil Erosion & Riverbed Mining (1 Mark):** Heavy deforestation from open-cast manganese mining along riverbanks causes severe siltation, reduces reservoir storage capacity, and threatens endemic river ecology.

#### Key Points
- **Over-Damming & Massive Forest Submergence:** Across its 184 km length flowing to the Arabian Sea, **6 major hydroelectric dams** have already submerged over **32,000 acres** of rich, biodiverse evergreen rainforests in Uttara Kannada. A proposed 7th dam threatened to drown another 210 hectares of virgin forest.

- **Toxic Industrial Pollution (Dandeli Paper Factory):** The 58-year-old **West Coast Paper Mills at Dandeli** continuously discharged untreated black liquor wastewater laden with toxic lignin, sulfides, and chlorine compounds directly into the Kali River, contaminating drinking water and poisoning aquatic life.

- **Civil Resistance & Expansion Rejection:** Led by **Panduranga Hegde** and the *Kali Bachao Andolan*, local communities fiercely opposed the proposed expansion of the Dandeli paper mill. The expansion was successfully rejected on 2 major grounds: 1) Government environmental clearance regulations / TERI EIA study, and 2) Unviable environmental and social costs incurred.

- **Siltation from Sand Mining & Mining Runoff:** Unregulated riverbed sand mining and open-cast manganese/iron mining in the catchment destroy riparian buffer zones, destabilize riverbanks, and choke benthic breeding grounds of endemic Western Ghats fish.

#### Detailed Answer
**Geographical Context:** The **Kali River** is a pristine 184-kilometer-long river originating in the Western Ghats at Kusavali village in Uttara Kannada district, Karnataka, flowing westward into the Arabian Sea near Karwar. It traverses one of the world's eight hottest biodiversity hotspots. However, cumulative anthropogenic pressures have turned the Kali River into a severely stressed watershed.

#### 1. Dam Cascades and Massive Rainforest Submergence

Despite measuring only 184 km in length, the Kali River basin has been intensely exploited for hydroelectric generation. **Six major hydroelectric dams** (including the Supa, Kodasalli, Kadra, Bommanahalli, Tattihalla, and Kaneri dams) were constructed in a cascade. These mega-reservoirs have submerged more than **32,000 acres** of ancient, dense tropical evergreen rainforests across Uttara Kannada, destroying critical wildlife corridors for elephants, tigers, and hornbills. Recently, a **7th dam was proposed** that threatened to submerge an additional 210 hectares of prime forest canopy, triggering immediate civil alarm.

#### 2. Severe Industrial Contamination from Dandeli Paper Factory

The river suffers intense industrial chemical pollution from the 58-year-old **West Coast Paper Mills** located at Dandeli. The manufacturing facility consumes enormous volumes of freshwater and timber pulp, discharging millions of liters of toxic, untreated or poorly treated pulp effluent directly into the river. This effluent contains toxic chlorinated phenols, heavy metals, sulfides, and black liquor, creating extreme biochemical oxygen demand (BOD) deficits. The river water turned dark and foul-smelling, triggering chronic skin ailments, digestive disorders among downstream communities, and mass fish kills.

#### 3. The Kali Bachao Andolan Campaign & Legal Victory

To resist this multi-pronged ecological destruction, environmentalist **Panduranga Hegde** and local civil society launched the **Kali Bachao Andolan** (Save Kali River Movement). When the 58-year-old Dandeli paper mill sought state permission for a major industrial expansion, the Andolan mobilized fishermen, farmers, and students, presenting rigorous scientific counter-evidence. Consequently, the government rejected the paper mill's expansion on two decisive grounds:

- **Government Notification & EIA Standards:** The Environmental Impact Assessment (EIA) conducted by **TERI (The Energy and Resources Institute)** demonstrated severe violations of statutory environmental standards and cumulative carrying capacity thresholds.

- **Cost Incurred vs. Ecological Loss:** The long-term economic, health, and ecological damages inflicted on public water resources far outweighed any private industrial profits.

#### 4. Unregulated Sand Mining and Catchment Siltation

In addition to industrial toxicity and dams, the Kali River basin suffers from widespread illegal **sand mining** along the riverbed and estuaries. Heavy mechanised dredging strips riverbanks of stabilizing roots, accelerates bank erosion, alters natural flow dynamics, and destroys sensitive benthic gravel beds where endemic fish lay eggs. Runoff from open-cast manganese and iron ore mines further silts the riverbed, degrading the river's self-cleansing capacity.

*Slide Reference: Class 13, Slide 10.*

### [EQB-13-05] List any 4 environmental movements with their location.

- **Topic:** Four Indian Grassroots Movements & Locations
- **Category:** `Short Answer & Definitions`
- **Marks / Importance:** 2M | HIGH (⭐⭐⭐⭐)
- **Exam Appearances:** End Exam 2019 (Section IX, Q1)

#### Expected Answer
**Exam Calibrated Answer: 2 Marks (0.5 Mark each)**

**Four Major Indian Environmental Movements and Locations:**

- **Bishnoi Movement (1730 AD):** Khejarli Village, Jodhpur District, **Rajasthan** (protecting Khejri trees).

- **Chipko Movement (1973):** Reni & Gopeshwar, Chamoli District, **Uttarakhand** (Garhwal Himalayas).

- **Appiko Movement (1983):** Sirsi, Uttara Kannada District, **Karnataka** (Western Ghats).

- **Silent Valley Movement (1978–1985):** Palakkad District, **Kerala** (Kunthipuzha river basin).

#### Key Points
- **1. Bishnoi Movement (1730 AD):** Khejarli village, Jodhpur district, Rajasthan (Western Thar Desert). Led by Amrita Devi Bishnoi sacrificing lives to protect sacred Khejri trees.

- **2. Chipko Movement (1973):** Gopeshwar and Reni village, Chamoli district, Uttarakhand (Garhwal Himalayas). Led by Chandi Prasad Bhatt, Gaura Devi, and Sunderlal Bahuguna.

- **3. Appiko Movement (1983):** Salkani and Husri forests, Sirsi, Uttara Kannada district, Karnataka (Western Ghats). Led by Pandurang Hegde.

- **4. Save Silent Valley Movement (1978–1985):** Palakkad district, Nilgiri Biosphere Reserve, Kerala (Kunthipuzha River valley). Mobilized by Kerala Sastra Sahitya Parishad (KSSP).

- *Additional Slide Movements:*
<br>• **Kali Bachao Andolan:** Kali River basin, Uttara Kannada, Karnataka (led by Panduranga Hegde against over-damming and Dandeli paper factory pollution).
<br>• **Narmada Bachao Andolan (1985):** Narmada River basin across Madhya Pradesh, Gujarat, and Maharashtra (led by Medha Patkar, Baba Amte, and Arundhati Roy).

#### Detailed Answer
**The Ethos of Indian Environmental Struggles:** In Western environmental history, early conservation often centered around preserving untouched wilderness for recreation, science, or wildlife sanctuaries. In India, grassroots environmental movements arose as struggles for **human survival, social justice, and basic resource rights**. Rural and tribal populations organized non-violent satyagraha campaigns to protect their traditional communal lifelines—water, forest, and land (*Jal, Jungle, Jameen*)—against state commercialization and destructive megaprojects.

#### Four Key Indian Environmental Movements from Class 13 Slides:

- **Bishnoi Movement (Khejarli Village, Rajasthan - 1730 AD):**
<br>Recognized as the world's earliest documented environmental martyrs and ecological stewards. Guided by the spiritual rules of Guru Jambheshwar (which strictly prohibit cutting green trees and mandate compassion for all living beings), **Amrita Devi Bishnoi** and her three young daughters physically hugged sacred Khejri (*Prosopis cineraria*) trees in 1730 to prevent soldiers of the Maharaja of Jodhpur from felling them for lime kilns. A total of 363 Bishnoi men, women, and children sacrificed their lives before the Maharaja ordered an immediate halt to tree cutting and established a permanent royal sanctuary.

- **Chipko Movement (Chamoli District, Uttarakhand - 1973):**
<br>Sparked in the Himalayan foothills when local mountain farmers in Gopeshwar were denied timber for basic agricultural plows while a commercial sports goods manufacturer received logging permits. Led by **Chandi Prasad Bhatt** (DGSS), **Gaura Devi** (Mahila Mangal Dal), and **Sunderlal Bahuguna**, villagers formed human cordons around trees in Reni forest. The movement asserted that *'Ecology is permanent economy'*, successfully securing a 15-year statutory commercial logging ban across the Uttarakhand Himalayas in 1980.

- **Appiko Movement (Uttara Kannada District, Karnataka - 1983):**
<br>Known as the southern counterpart to Chipko ('Appiko' means 'to hug' in Kannada). Initiated in the Western Ghats forests of Salkani and Husri by youth leader **Pandurang Hegde** to halt state clear-felling for commercial timber, plywood factories, and teak monoculture. The movement followed a clear three-pronged framework: **Preservation** (*Ubsu*), **Restoration** (*Belesu* via the 5F framework: Food, Fodder, Fuel, Fertilizer, Fiber), and **Rational Use** (*Balasu*).

- **Save Silent Valley Movement (Palakkad District, Kerala - 1978–1985):**
<br>A groundbreaking environmental battle to protect 8.3 square kilometers of ancient, untouched tropical moist evergreen rainforest along the Kunthipuzha River from submergence by a proposed 120 MW hydroelectric dam. Spearheaded by the **Kerala Sastra Sahitya Parishad (KSSP)** using citizen science, street theater, and ecology education, the campaign safeguarded the endangered Lion-tailed macaque (*Macaca silenus*) and culminated in the creation of the Silent Valley National Park in 1985.

#### Notable River Basin Movements from the Lecture:

The slides also feature two major anti-dam and river protection struggles: **Kali Bachao Andolan** in Uttara Kannada, Karnataka (opposing 6 existing dams and pollution from the Dandeli paper mill), and **Narmada Bachao Andolan** across Gujarat, Madhya Pradesh, and Maharashtra (led by Medha Patkar, Baba Amte, and Arundhati Roy against mega-dams like Sardar Sarovar).

*Slide Reference: Class 13, Slides 2, 4, 8, 9, 10, 11.*

### [EQB-13-06] Describe the Appiko movement in Karnataka and the Silent Valley movement in Kerala.

- **Topic:** Appiko & Silent Valley Movements Detailed Case Study
- **Category:** `Descriptive & Case Study`
- **Marks / Importance:** 4M | HIGH (⭐⭐⭐⭐)
- **Exam Appearances:** End Exam 2019 (Section IX), End Exam 2018 (Part I)

#### Expected Answer
**Exam Calibrated Answer: 4 Marks (2 Marks per movement)**

- **Appiko Movement (Karnataka) (2 Marks):**
  
    <li>*Origin & Leadership (1M):* Started in September 1983 in Salkani forest (Sirsi, Uttara Kannada) led by **Pandurang Hegde**; inspired by Chipko (*Appiko* means "to hug" in Kannada).

    - *Objectives & Impact (1M):* Protested commercial monoculture tree felling and paper mill over-exploitation in Western Ghats. Achieved strict government bans on felling green trees in catchment forests based on three goals: *Ubsu* (save), *Belesu* (grow), and *Balasu* (use sustainably).

  

</li>
- **Silent Valley Movement (Kerala) (2 Marks):**
  
    <li>*Origin & Leadership (1M):* Begun in 1978 by Kerala Sastra Sahithya Parishad (**KSSP**) against a proposed 120 MW hydroelectric dam across the **Kunthipuzha River** in Palakkad district.

    - *Ecological Value & Outcome (1M):* Defended one of India's last undisturbed evergreen tropical rainforests and the primary habitat of the endangered **Lion-tailed Macaque**. Successfully persuaded the Central Government to scrap the dam project in 1983, declaring Silent Valley a National Park in 1984.

  

</li>

#### Key Points
- **Appiko Movement (1983 - Western Ghats, Karnataka):**
<br>• *Meaning & Origin:* 'Appiko' means 'to embrace/hug' in Kannada—the direct southern counterpart to Chipko, initiated in September 1983 in Salkani and Husri forests of Uttara Kannada.
<br>• *Root Cause:* Severe deforestation dating back to 1969 when 900 acres of natural forest in Husri village were clear-felled, drying up perennial streams and eroding soils.
<br>• *Leader & Mobilization:* Led by **Pandurang Hegde**; villagers hugged trees to halt commercial felling by contractors for paper mills and plywood industries.
<br>• *Threefold Conservation Strategy:* 1) **Preservation** (*Ubsu* - saving remaining natural forests), 2) **Restoration** (*Belesu* - regenerating denuded land with **5F species**: Food, Fodder, Fuel, Fertilizer, Fiber), 3) **Rational Use** (*Balasu* - sustainable harvesting and reducing fuel wood pressure using improved chulhas and biogas).
<br>• *Regional Reach:* Extended across Uttara Kannada, Kodagu, and the wider Western Ghats belt.

- **Save Silent Valley Movement (1978–1985 - Palakkad, Kerala):**
<br>• *The Threat:* In 1970, the Kerala State Electricity Board (KSEB) proposed a 120 MW hydroelectric dam across the **Kunthipuzha River**, which would submerge 8.3 sq km of ancient, virgin tropical moist evergreen rainforest.
<br>• *Ecological Alarm:* Biologists Steven Green and Rom Whitaker (BNHS) identified Silent Valley as one of the last viable habitats for the endangered **Lion-tailed macaque** (*Macaca silenus*). Botanists proved the forest was an irreplaceable evolutionary reservoir.
<br>• *Citizen Science & KSSP:* The **Kerala Sastra Sahitya Parishad (KSSP)** led a statewide mass awareness campaign using popular science literature, street theater, and village seminars, proving the dam's power output was negligible compared to the permanent destruction of biodiversity.
<br>• *Legal & Political Milestones:* N.V. Krishna secured a High Court stay order in August 1979; Prime Ministers Morarji Desai, Charan Singh, and Indira Gandhi ordered independent reviews; Indira Gandhi declared Silent Valley protected in January 1981; the hydel project was formally called off in November 1983; and Prime Minister Rajiv Gandhi inaugurated Silent Valley National Park in 1985.

#### Detailed Answer
**Context:** While North India witnessed the Chipko movement in the Himalayas, South India's Western Ghats—one of the world's premier biodiversity hotspots—gave birth to two foundational ecological movements: the **Appiko Movement** in Karnataka and the **Silent Valley Movement** in Kerala. Together, they demonstrated how grassroots communities, scientists, and civil society could reverse commercial deforestation and destructive river valley projects.

#### Part A: The Appiko Movement in Karnataka (Slide 8)

**1. Background and Genesis:** In Kannada, the word *Appiko* literally translates to 'to hug' or 'to embrace'. During the 1960s and 1970s, the Karnataka State Forest Department prioritized revenue-driven commercial forestry. In 1969, over **900 acres of natural forest in Husri village** were clear-felled to establish monoculture plantations of teak and eucalyptus for paper and plywood industries. This commercial exploitation led to severe ecological consequences: perennial mountain springs dried up, water tables plummeted, fertile soils washed away, and local villagers were stripped of essential cattle fodder and fuelwood.

**2. Direct Action in Salkani (1983):** In September 1983, inspired by the Himalayan Chipko Movement, rural youth leader **Pandurang Hegde** mobilized the villagers of Salkani and Husri in Uttara Kannada district. When commercial logging contractors and forest officials arrived with axes, men, women, and children walked into the forest, hugged the trees, and refused to leave. This direct action forced contractors to withdraw their axes and brought commercial clear-felling to an immediate standstill.

**3. The Threefold Operational Strategy:** Unlike purely reactive protests, Appiko formulated a comprehensive, positive constructive methodology known as the *Three 'Ubs'/'Bel'/'Bal'* principles:

- **Preservation (*Ubsu* in Kannada):** Actively defending existing natural forest canopies from commercial tree felling, contract auctions, and illicit logging through vigilant community patrols.

- **Restoration (*Belesu* in Kannada):** Regenerating deforested and barren common lands through community afforestation centered on the **5F Framework**—planting native species that yield **Food (fruits/nuts), Fodder (cattle forage), Fuel (dry deadwood), Fertilizer (green manure for agricultural fields), and Fiber (for handicrafts and rope)**.

- **Rational Use (*Balasu* in Kannada):** Reducing overall human pressure on forest resources by introducing fuel-efficient cookstoves (improved *chulhas* that use 50% less wood), constructing household biogas digesters, and establishing sustainable harvesting rotations for non-timber forest produce (NTFPs).

**4. Spread and Regional Impact:** Pandurang Hegde and the Appiko activists initiated dialogue with state forest officials, organizing joint forest walks so officials could inspect the destruction caused by timber contractors. The movement spread rapidly across Karnataka, notably impacting Kodagu (Coorg), Shimoga, and neighboring districts, forcing the state government to halt the commercial clear-cutting of natural evergreen forests.

#### Part B: The Save Silent Valley Movement in Kerala (Slide 9)

**1. The Threat to the Kunthipuzha Valley:** Silent Valley, located in the Palakkad district of Kerala, encompasses one of the oldest, undisturbed, closed-canopy tropical moist evergreen rainforests on the planet, drained by the pristine, perennial **Kunthipuzha River**. In 1970, the **Kerala State Electricity Board (KSEB)** unveiled a major hydroelectric proposal: a dam across the Kunthipuzha River to generate 120 MW of power and irrigate agricultural lands. However, the project's reservoir would have permanently submerged **8.3 square kilometers** of virgin rainforest, drowning ancient evolutionary gene pools.

**2. Ecological and Scientific Alarms (1971–1976):** In 1971–1972, primatologist **Steven Green** and conservationist **Rom Whitaker** of the Bombay Natural History Society (BNHS) conducted field studies showing that Silent Valley harbored one of the last viable breeding populations of the globally endangered **Lion-tailed macaque** (*Macaca silenus*). Botanists and evolutionary biologists emphasized that the valley was an unfragmented relic ecosystem containing rare medicinal flora and wild ancestors of cultivated crops. In October 1976, the National Committee on Environment Planning and Coordination (NCEPC) established a task force chaired by **Zafar Futehally** to evaluate the ecological trade-offs.

**3. Citizen Science and the Kerala Sastra Sahitya Parishad (KSSP):** What distinguished the Silent Valley struggle was its transformation into a broad-based, non-violent mass movement driven by science education. In 1977, ecologist **Sathish Chandran Nair** surveyed the valley, sounding alarms with environmentalist **K.P. Vijayan** and reaching out to Prime Minister Morarji Desai. The **Kerala Sastra Sahitya Parishad (KSSP)**—a progressive voluntary science organization—stepped in. KSSP organized village-level study groups, street plays, poetry recitals, and public lectures across Kerala. Their objective techno-economic analysis proved that the 120 MW of power produced was marginal, could be generated via alternative thermal or transmission-efficiency measures, and in no way justified erasing an irreplaceable biological treasure.

**4. Legal Interventions and Chronological Victory (1979–1985):**

- *June–August 1979:* Despite growing protests, the Kerala government began preparatory road construction. In August 1979, activist **N.V. Krishna** and nature lovers filed a writ petition in the High Court of Kerala, securing an urgent stay order, while Prime Minister Charan Singh directed a fresh central review.

- *January 1980:* Although the High Court eventually dismissed the writ petition citing technical administrative boundaries, public resistance had grown unstoppable.

- *January 1981:* Bowing to relentless nationwide civil society pressure, Prime Minister **Indira Gandhi** intervened and announced that Silent Valley would be protected from industrial submergence.

- *November 1983:* The Kerala government and KSEB officially scrapped the Silent Valley Hydroelectric Project.

- *1985:* Prime Minister **Rajiv Gandhi** formally inaugurated the **Silent Valley National Park**, permanently safeguarding this pristine ecosystem for future generations.

#### Comparative Summary

| **Year & Region** | 1983; Uttara Kannada & Kodagu (Western Ghats) | 1978–1985; Palakkad District (Western Ghats) 

| **Core Threat** | Commercial clear-felling for paper/plywood mills & monoculture | Submergence of 8.3 sq km rainforest by 120 MW hydel dam 

| **Key Leaders / Orgs** | Pandurang Hegde, rural youth, local village communities | KSSP, Steven Green, Rom Whitaker, Sathish Chandran Nair, N.V. Krishna 

| **Primary Weapon** | Embracing trees (satyagraha) & 5F afforestation (Food, Fodder, Fuel, Fertilizer, Fiber) | Citizen science, ecological literacy, PIL in High Court, public advocacy 

| **Final Outcome** | Ban on commercial clear-felling; widespread ecological restoration | Cancellation of hydel project; declared Silent Valley National Park (1985) 

*Slide Reference: Class 13, Slides 8, 9.*

## Class 14: Green Computing

### [EQB-14-01] Define Green Computing. Explain the four fundamental approaches across the hardware lifecycle: Green Design, Green Manufacturing, Green Use, and Green Disposal.

- **Topic:** Green Computing Definition & 4 Core Lifecycle Approaches
- **Category:** `Comprehensive Descriptive`
- **Marks / Importance:** 2M – 10M | CRITICAL (⭐⭐⭐⭐⭐)
- **Exam Appearances:** EST End Exam 2024 (Section IV, Q(e)), End Exam 2019 (Section VI, Q1), End Exam 2018 (Part II, Q1–2)

#### Expected Answer
**Exam Calibrated Answer: 2M – 10M**

**[For 2 Marks — Definition & 4 Fundamental Approaches (San Murugesan)]:**

**Green Computing** is the study and practice of designing, manufacturing, using, and disposing of computers, servers, and associated subsystems efficiently and effectively with minimal or zero impact on the environment.

**The Four Pillars (0.5M each):**

- **Green Design:** Engineering energy-efficient, hazardous-material-free computers, cooling systems, and algorithms.

- **Green Manufacturing:** Fabricating electronic components with minimal waste, non-toxic inputs, and low carbon emissions.

- **Green Use:** Minimizing electricity consumption during operation (sleep modes, power management, server virtualization).

- **Green Disposal:** Reusing, refurbishing, and properly recycling obsolete hardware under EPR without landfilling.

**[If asked for 5M – 10M, add Detailed Architectural Measures]:**

- **Energy Star & Power Consumption (2 Marks):** Transitioning from CRT monitors (80W) to LCDs (35W) and LEDs (25W); deploying 80-Plus certified power supplies with $>80\%$ conversion efficiency.

- **Server Virtualization & Consolidation (2 Marks):** Running multiple logical virtual machines on a single physical host server, raising CPU utilization from typical 5–15% to 60–80%, cutting hardware footprint and chiller cooling loads by $>50\%$.

- **Algorithm & Software Greening (1 Mark):** Designing computational algorithms with optimized time complexity ($O(n)$ vs $O(n^2)$) to reduce processor cycle duration and associated joules/kWh consumed per transaction.

#### Key Points
- **Definition:** Green computing is the environmentally responsible and eco-friendly use of computers and their resources. It is the comprehensive study and practice of designing, manufacturing, using, and disposing of computing devices in such a way that reduces environmental impact.

- **Origins:** The term was coined shortly after 1992, when the US Environmental Protection Agency (EPA) launched the **Energy Star** labeling program to recognize energy efficiency in monitors and climate control equipment.

- **The 4 Core Lifecycle Approaches:**
<br>1. **Green Design:** Engineering energy-efficient processors, passive cooling, modular architectures, non-toxic recyclable materials, and prioritizing laptops over desktops.
<br>2. **Green Manufacturing:** Eliminating hazardous toxic chemicals in fabrication to prevent them entering water and food chains, minimizing cleanroom energy/water, and implementing eco-packaging.
<br>3. **Green Use:** Slashing operational power through server virtualization, intelligent power management (sleep modes, powering off idle CPUs/fans), selecting low-power monitors (LED 25W vs LCD 35W vs CRT 80W), Energy Star ratings (80% vs 70% efficiency), and optimizing algorithmic efficiency (e.g. Google 0.20g vs AOL 0.37g CO2 per query).
<br>4. **Green Disposal:** Managing 20–50 million tons of annual global e-waste (~5% of total planetary waste) through responsible reuse, refurbishing, and certified material recycling to eliminate informal toxic dumping.

#### Detailed Answer
**Definition & Historical Context:** Green computing refers to environmentally sustainable computing. It represents a paradigm shift in designing computer systems that evaluates processing performance alongside energy efficiency. The term itself was coined shortly after 1992, when the US Environmental Protection Agency (EPA) launched the **Energy Star** program—a voluntary labeling initiative engineered to promote and recognize energy efficiency in computer monitors, power supplies, and climate control equipment.

The imperative for green computing stems from the massive ecological footprint of Information and Communications Technology (ICT), which currently accounts for **2% of total global CO2 emissions**. Computational energy is frequently squandered by leaving machines active when idle (where CPU, fans, and screen savers consume continuous power) and by data centers operating with inadequate cooling capacities. Furthermore, the fabrication, packaging, and disposal of computing devices release hazardous persistent pollutants into ecosystems.

#### The Four Fundamental Lifecycle Approaches

As established in the core curriculum framework (Murugesan lifecycle model), green computing must address every stage of a computing device's lifespan:

  - **Green Design (Pre-Production & Engineering):**
    Focuses on architecting environmentally sound computing machinery before fabrication begins. Key strategies include designing microprocessors with advanced low-power sleep states, reducing thermal design power (TDP), implementing passive conduction cooling to eliminate electromechanical fans, and selecting biodegradable or non-toxic materials (e.g. bio-resins, cornstarch polymers, and screwless chassis). Slide 10 emphasizes hardware form-factor selection: laptops should always be preferred over desktop computers because their mobile-optimized architectures draw significantly less wattage.

  

  - **Green Manufacturing (Fabrication & Assembly):**
    Addresses the industrial fabrication of microchips, printed circuit boards (PCBs), and peripherals. Semiconductor fabrication facilities consume massive amounts of process water—often equivalent to the water consumption of an entire small city—and generate toxic chemical effluents. Green manufacturing mandates eliminating hazardous substances (such as lead, mercury, cadmium, and hexavalent chromium) to prevent toxic chemicals from leaching into groundwater and entering the human food chain. It incorporates Environmentally Preferable Purchasing (EPP) standards and cleanroom energy minimization.

  

  - **Green Use (Operational Efficiency & Software):**
    Minimizes electrical consumption during the active deployment of IT infrastructure across several domains:

    
      <li>*Virtualization:* The process of running two or more logical computer systems on one physical set of hardware. Virtualization platforms can span hundreds of interconnected physical computers and storage devices to create an entire virtual infrastructure. This slashes an enterprise's power and cooling consumption by dramatically reducing the physical machine footprint.

      - *Power Management & User Habits:* Turning off computers and printers when not in use, enabling automated sleep modes, and recognizing that screen savers consume active electrical power rather than saving it.

      - *Monitor Technology Selection:* Operational power varies drastically across display technologies: a 17-inch CRT monitor consumes **80 Watts**; a 17-inch LCD monitor consumes **35 Watts** (preferred for general users); and a 17-inch LED monitor consumes only **25 Watts** (though historically rare and expensive). Furthermore, an Energy Star rated computer operates at **80% energy efficiency**, compared to only **70% efficiency** for an unrated machine.

      - *Algorithmic Efficiency:* Software algorithms directly impact the hardware resources and electrical energy consumed per computational task. Slide 11 documents the carbon cost per internet search:
        <ul>
          <li>Google Search: $\approx 0.20\text{ g } \text{CO}_2$

          - Yahoo Search: $\approx 0.26\text{ g } \text{CO}_2$

          - Microsoft Live Search: $\approx 0.32\text{ g } \text{CO}_2$

          - AOL Search: $\approx 0.37\text{ g } \text{CO}_2$

        

      </li>
    </ul>
  </li>
  - **Green Disposal (End-of-Life & Resource Recovery):**
    Addresses post-consumer hardware retirement. Global e-waste generation stands at **20 to 50 million tons per year**, representing approximately **5% of all solid waste on the planet**. Green disposal establishes systematic frameworks for e-waste handling: prioritizing component refurbishment, secondary market reuse, and environmentally sound material recycling (urban mining of precious metals) to prevent hazardous heavy metals from polluting soil and drinking water reservoirs.

  

#### Lifecycle Engineering Summary Matrix

  
  
    
      | **Green Design** 
      | R&D / Architecture 
      | Low-power CPU logic, passive cooling, screwless modular chassis, laptop form factors 
      | Laptops consume up to 70% less power than desktop towers 
    

    
      | **Green Manufacturing** 
      | Foundry / Cleanroom 
      | Closed-loop water treatment, EPP guidelines, eliminating toxic heavy metals 
      | Prevents fab effluents equivalent to small city water demand from polluting watersheds 
    

    
      | **Green Use** 
      | Active Deployment 
      | Server virtualization, sleep modes, display upgrades (LED 25W vs CRT 80W), efficient algorithms 
      | Energy Star cuts power loss (80% vs 70% efficiency); Google search cuts carbon to 0.20g CO2 
    

    
      | **Green Disposal** 
      | Retirement / EOL 
      | Refurbishing, component reuse, closed-loop material recycling, certified recovery 
      | Diverts 20–50 million tons/year of e-waste (5% of planetary waste) from toxic landfills 
    

  

*Slide Reference: Class 14, Slides 6, 7, 8, 9, 10, 11, and 19.*
