### [EQB-14-02] Differentiate thoroughly between Thick Clients and Thin Clients. Compare their system architecture, operational energy, embodied resources, hardware lifespan, purchase cost, and environmental footprint. Include the Queen Margaret University case study.

- **Topic:** Thick Client vs Thin Client Architecture
- **Category:** `Differentiate Between`
- **Marks / Importance:** 2M – 4M | CRITICAL (⭐⭐⭐⭐⭐)
- **Exam Appearances:** EST End Exam 2024 (Section IV, Q(a)), End Exam 2023 (Section IV, Q3), End Exam 2022 (Section VII, Q(a)), End Exam 2019 (Section VI, Q2)

#### Expected Answer
**Exam Calibrated Answer: 2M – 4M**

**[For 2 Marks — Two Core Differences (1M each)]:**

  | **Power Consumption (1M)** 
  | High ($75\text{ to }100\text{ W}$); contains internal hard drive, powerful CPU, cooling fans, and dedicated GPU. 
  | Very Low ($5\text{ to }15\text{ W}$); diskless solid-state terminal relying on centralized data center servers for processing. 

  | **Lifespan & E-Waste (1M)** 
  | Short ($3\text{ to }5\text{ years}$); rapid obsolescence requiring frequent hardware replacement. 
  | Long ($7\text{ to }10\text{ years}$); minimal physical wear, generating $>70\%$ less hardware e-waste over lifecycle. 

**[If asked for 4 Marks, add]:**

- **Capital & Operating Cost (1 Mark):** Thick clients cost $\sim £400\text{ to }£600$ per seat with high IT maintenance; thin clients cost $\sim £210$ per unit with centralized, automated software patching.

- **Life-Cycle Ecological Footprint (Slide 16 Case Study) (1 Mark):** Over lifecycle, a thick client embodies $3,244\text{ MJ}$ energy, $920\text{ L}$ water, and $193\text{ kg CO}_2\text{e}$; a thin client uses only $878\text{ MJ}$ energy and $56\text{ kg CO}_2\text{e}$ (saving $£50,000$ and $46\text{ t CO}_2\text{e}$ in Queen Margaret University trial).

#### Key Points
- **System Architecture:**
<br>• *Thin Client:* A small, silent device with no hard drives or internal moving parts and minimal local processing/RAM. It sends user inputs to a centralized server, which executes all applications and returns an updated display image to the terminal screen.
<br>• *Thick Client (Office PC):* A self-contained computer equipped with local hard drives, powerful multi-core CPU, dedicated memory, and local OS; operates independently without requiring a network server connection.

- **Operational Power Consumption:** Thick client desktop draws **75 Watts**; Thin client draws only **5 Watts** (slashing direct electricity demand by **over 93%**).

- **Capital Purchase Cost & Lifespan:**
<br>• Thick Client: Low-spec PC costs £400 with an expected lifespan of **5 years**.
<br>• Thin Client: Terminal unit costs £210 with an expected lifespan of **7 years**.

- **Resource & Material Footprint:**
<br>• Producing a standard PC and 17" monitor requires **20 kg of materials**, consumes **3,244 MJ of energy** and **920 liters of process water**, and emits **193 kg of greenhouse gases**.
<br>• Over its lifecycle, a PC and monitor generate **37 kg of non-hazardous waste** and **0.7 kg of hazardous waste**.
<br>• Thin clients require substantially fewer raw materials (plastics, precious metals), use less process water, and generate negligible heat.

- **Real-World Case Study:** Queen Margaret University (Edinburgh) saved **£50,000 in energy costs alone** at the halfway stage of migrating to thin client terminals.

- **Trade-Offs:** Thin clients depend entirely on 24x7 centralized servers, high network reliability, and software licensing costs; thick clients function autonomously offline.

#### Detailed Answer
**Architectural Paradigm:** In corporate, academic, and banking IT deployments, computing architecture fundamentally dictates energy demand and e-waste generation. The lecture slides establish a rigorous contrast between traditional distributed workstations (Thick Clients) and centralized virtualization terminals (Thin Clients):

  - **Thin Clients:** Defined as small, silent solid-state devices that communicate over a network with a centralized server to deliver a computing experience largely identical to that of a standalone PC. They contain *no mechanical hard drives or internal moving parts*, have minimal processing power with a relatively small quantity of RAM, and store no user applications locally. All data processing occurs on the central server, which streams updated display frames back to the terminal screen.

  - **Thick Clients:** Conventional desktop personal computers that do not require an active server connection to run. While they benefit from network connectivity for shared storage or internet access, they process instructions, run operating systems, and store files locally and independently.

#### Deep Comparative Engineering Matrix

  
  
    
      | **Internal Moving Parts** 
      | Mechanical cooling fans, spinning magnetic HDDs, optical drives 
      | **Zero moving parts**; solid-state fanless conduction 
    

    
      | **Operational Power Draw** 
      | **75 Watts** (active baseline) 
      | **5 Watts** (slashes operational power by 93.3%) 
    

    
      | **Purchase Cost** 
      | **£400** (for a low-specification office PC) 
      | **£210** (terminal cost) 
    

    
      | **Hardware Useful Lifespan** 
      | **5 years** (rapid hardware obsolescence) 
      | **7 years** (processing upgrades happen centrally) 
    

    
      | **Embodied Production Energy** 
      | **3,244 Mega Joules (MJ)** per unit and monitor 
      | Drastically lower embodied energy (small lightweight chassis) 
    

    
      | **Manufacturing Process Water** 
      | **920 liters** of process water consumed 
      | Minimal water consumption due to reduced silicon/board area 
    

    
      | **Material Requirement** 
      | **20 kg** of physical materials (PC + 17" monitor) 
      | Minimal plastics, copper, and precious metals 
    

    
      | **Lifecycle Solid Waste** 
      | **37 kg non-hazardous** + **0.7 kg hazardous waste** 
      | Fraction of desktop e-waste mass; highly recyclable 
    

    
      | **Greenhouse Gas Emissions** 
      | **193 kg of GHGs** generated during manufacturing 
      | Massive net carbon reduction across entire lifecycle 
    

    
      | **Thermal Load & Noise** 
      | Emits substantial heat; strains office HVAC; noisy fans 
      | **Completely silent**; negligible heat dissipation 
    

    
      | **Server & Network Dependency** 
      | Independent; functions offline without server connectivity 
      | 100% dependent on continuous 24x7 active server availability 
    

  

#### Environmental & Financial Case Study: Queen Margaret University

Slide 16 details the landmark institutional deployment at **Queen Margaret University in Edinburgh**. By replacing conventional thick client workstations with low-power thin client terminals, the university saved **£50,000 in direct electricity costs alone** at the halfway stage of implementation. The energy reduction eliminated vast amounts of waste heat, thereby reducing secondary electrical loads from office and lab air-conditioning systems.

#### Limitations & Advantages of Thick Clients

Despite the overwhelming environmental advantages of thin clients, the slides document specific architectural caveats:

  - Thin client infrastructure does not always deliver equal computing efficiency for compute-intensive tasks (such as 3D rendering or local compile jobs).

  - Deployment involves substantial backend capital expenditure: acquiring enterprise server racks, high-capacity centralized storage, network switching gear, and expensive multi-user software licenses.

  - The primary architectural advantage of the Thick Client is autonomy: it requires no central server connection, whereas thin clients demand that centralized virtual servers remain powered on **24 hours a day, 7 days a week (24 x 7)**.

*Slide Reference: Class 14, Slides 14, 15, and 16.*

### [EQB-14-03] What is Moore's law? Explain how it drives rapid technological obsolescence and accelerates the global e-waste crisis.

- **Topic:** Moore's Law & Electronic Waste Surge
- **Category:** `Short Answer & Definitions`
- **Marks / Importance:** 1M | CRITICAL (⭐⭐⭐⭐⭐)
- **Exam Appearances:** EST End Exam 2024 (Section II, Q(e)), End Exam 2023 (Section I, Q1(a))

#### Expected Answer
**Exam Calibrated Answer: 1 Mark**

**Moore's Law & E-Waste Generation (1 Mark):**

Coined by Gordon Moore (1965), **Moore's Law** observes that the number of transistors on a microchip doubles approximately every 18 to 24 months, doubling computing power at decreasing cost. In the environmental context, this exponential performance curve accelerates planned software obsolescence, reducing hardware replacement cycles to just **2 to 3 years** and driving the generation of over **50 million tonnes of toxic e-waste globally each year**.

#### Key Points
- **Moore's Law Definition:** An empirical prediction formulated by Gordon Moore (co-founder of Intel) stating that computer processing power roughly doubles every two years.

- **Technological Obsolescence:** Rapid exponential growth in microchip density causes state-of-the-art computing equipment to become functionally obsolete within 2–3 years, driving premature hardware replacement long before physical component failure.

- **Global E-Waste Surge:** According to the United Nations Environment Programme (UNEP), approximately **50 million tons of electronic waste (e-trash)** are generated globally each year (~4,000 tonnes discarded every single hour).

- **Resource & Carbon Burden:** Information and Communications Technology (ICT) accounts for **2% of global CO2 emissions**. Rapid obsolescence escalates destructive mineral mining, requiring around 35 distinct minerals (including copper, lead, zinc, tin, nickel, barium, and gold) for smartphone manufacturing alone.

#### Detailed Answer
**Definition & Historical Context:** Moore's Law originated from an observation by Gordon Moore, co-founder of Intel Corporation. Moore predicted that computer processing power—governed by the density of transistors packed onto an integrated silicon circuit—roughly doubles every two years. For over five decades, this empirical trajectory served as the foundational roadmap for the global semiconductor industry, fueling exponential gains in microchip performance and steep declines in per-unit computing costs.

#### The Obsolescence Trap & Environmental Repercussions

While Moore's Law catalyzed the modern digital revolution, the lecture slides highlight its profound negative ecological consequence: **rapid technological obsolescence**. Because processor performance doubles every 24 months, software developers continuously engineer increasingly resource-heavy operating systems and applications. Consequently, perfectly functional, undamaged computing hardware is rendered prematurely obsolete within 24 to 36 months simply because it cannot execute newer software workloads.

This rapid obsolescence cycle creates severe global environmental crises:

  - **Avalanche of Global E-Waste:** According to figures cited from the United Nations Environment Programme (UNEP) in Slide 5, approximately **50 million tons of electronic waste** are generated worldwide each year. Discarded electronics constitute roughly **5% of all solid waste on Earth**, with as much as **4,000 tonnes of toxic e-waste discarded every single hour** (Slide 20).

  - **Intensive Resource Extraction:** As detailed in Slide 2, modern technology is extremely mineral-intensive. A single smartphone requires approximately **35 different raw materials**. After silicon, plastic is the second most-used material. The finished devices contain significant quantities of iron, aluminium, copper, lead, zinc, tin, nickel, and barium. Mining these ores causes widespread deforestation, landscape degradation, heavy metal water contamination, and massive releases of CO and CO2 into the atmosphere from heavy machinery and smelting fuels.

  - **Embodied Carbon & Energy Footprint:** Global ICT infrastructure accounts for **2% of total worldwide CO2 emissions**. Because manufacturing microprocessors requires orders of magnitude more energy than operating them, discarding machines every two years multiplies the upstream fossil fuel and cleanroom manufacturing burden.

*Slide Reference: Class 14, Slides 2, 5, 11, and 20.*

### [EQB-14-04] Is e-waste hazardous? Explain the legal and chemical distinction, and describe how non-hazardous electronic goods become acute environmental and health hazards.

- **Topic:** Hazardous Nature of E-Waste
- **Category:** `Short Answer & Definitions`
- **Marks / Importance:** 1M – 2M | HIGH (⭐⭐⭐⭐)
- **Exam Appearances:** End Exam 2023 (Section I, Q2), End Exam 2018 (Part II, Q3)

#### Expected Answer
**Exam Calibrated Answer: 1M – 2M**

**[For 1 Mark — Intact vs Damaged State]:**

**E-waste is not hazardous per-se while intact** during normal consumer operation because toxic heavy metals (lead, mercury, cadmium) and halogenated flame retardants are safely sealed inside components. However, e-waste becomes **acutely hazardous** when dismantled, crushed, acid-leached, or open-burned in the informal recycling sector, releasing bioaccumulative toxins into air, soil, and water.

**[If asked for 2 Marks, add Specific Toxic Constituents (1M)]:**

- **Lead ($\text{Pb}$):** In CRT solder and circuit boards, causing irreversible neurotoxicity and brain damage.

- **Cadmium ($\text{Cd}$):** In SMD chip resistors and rechargeable Ni-Cd batteries, causing severe kidney damage.

- **Mercury ($\text{Hg}$):** In CCFL backlight tubes, causing chronic neurological and reproductive damage.

#### Key Points
- **Core Definition:** Electronic Waste (e-waste) refers to discarded electronic or electrical equipment that is no longer fit for its originally intended use (e.g. computers, cellular phones, stereos, refrigerators, air conditioners, and consumer durables).

- **Is E-Waste Hazardous?:** *e-waste is not hazardous waste per-se.* Intact electronic goods resting on an office desk or in home storage pose no immediate toxicity or radiation danger.

- **How It Becomes Hazardous:** The hazardous constituents present inside electronic components render the waste highly hazardous when devices are **dismantled, smashed, chemically stripped, or incinerated** during end-of-life processing.

- **Key Toxic Constituents & Sources:**
<br>• Lead & Cadmium: Printed circuit boards (solder) and CRT monitors (lead oxide funnel glass).
<br>• Mercury: Tilt switches and fluorescent backlights in flat-screen monitors.
<br>• Cadmium: Rechargeable computer batteries and SMD chip resistors.
<br>• Polychlorinated Biphenyls (PCBs): Capacitors and transformers.
<br>• Brominated Flame Retardants (BFRs): Circuit boards and plastic outer casings.
<br>• Polyvinyl Chloride (PVC): Cable insulation and wiring coatings.

- **Environmental Pathways:** Informal backyard acid leaching and open burning release toxic ash, heavy metals, and persistent organic pollutants into ambient air, soil, groundwater, and local agricultural food chains.

#### Detailed Answer
**The Precise Legal & Physical Distinction:** Slide 20 addresses the fundamental legal and environmental question: *"Is e-waste hazardous?"* The lecture slides provide the authoritative answer: **"e-waste is not hazardous waste per-se."**

When an electronic device—such as a personal computer, television, or cellular phone—is intact and in operational service, its internal toxic chemicals are safely encapsulated inside solid matrices, chip packages, and insulated housings. An intact computer resting on a worker's desk does not expose occupants to chemical toxins. However, Slide 20 emphasizes the critical caveat: *"the hazardous constituents present in the e-waste render it hazardous when such wastes are dismantled and processed, and pose hazard to health and environment."*

#### Toxic Chemical Constituents in Discarded Electronics

As cataloged in Slide 21, electronic components contain a wide array of heavy metals, halogens, and persistent synthetic compounds:

  
  
    
      | **Printed Circuit Boards (PCBs)** 
      | Lead (Pb) & Cadmium (Cd) 
      | Open solder torching releases lead fumes; acid stripping discharges dissolved cadmium into open drains. 
    

    
      | **Cathode Ray Tubes (CRTs)** 
      | Lead oxide & Cadmium 
      | Cracking funnel glass releases lead-laden phosphor dust, causing soil contamination and groundwater leaching. 
    

    
      | **Switches & Flat Screen Monitors** 
      | Mercury (Hg) 
      | Cold cathode fluorescent backlights break easily, volatilizing elemental mercury vapor that bioaccumulates as neurotoxic methylmercury. 
    

    
      | **Computer Batteries** 
      | Cadmium (Cd) 
      | Corrosion in unlined dumps leaches cadmium into aquifers, damaging kidneys and causing bone demineralization. 
    

    
      | **Capacitors and Transformers** 
      | Polychlorinated Biphenyls (PCBs) 
      | Dielectric oil leakage causes persistent environmental contamination; potent endocrine disruptor and liver toxin. 
    

    
      | **Circuit Boards & Plastic Casings** 
      | Brominated Flame Retardants (BFRs) 
      | Thermal combustion releases polybrominated dibenzodioxins and furans; bioaccumulates in adipose tissue. 
    

    
      | **Cable Insulation / Coating** 
      | Polyvinyl Chloride (PVC) 
      | Open burning over tire fires to harvest internal copper releases carcinogenic chlorinated dioxins into local airsheds. 
    

  

#### Environmental Transport Pathways

When discarded electronics enter informal recycling streams—concentrated across developing nations such as India, China, and Ghana—they are subjected to crude backyard operations: hammer smashing, open-air acid bath leaching, and wire burning. These crude practices liberate heavy metals and organic toxins, creating toxic ash plumes that contaminate municipal air, infiltrate groundwater tables, and enter agricultural food systems (as demonstrated by PCB and dioxin contamination of poultry eggs in Ghana, Slide 24).

*Slide Reference: Class 14, Slides 20, 21, 23, and 24.*

### [EQB-14-05] What is the Basel Convention? Explain its core objective in governing transboundary movement of e-waste and its relevance to developing nations.

- **Topic:** Basel Convention on Hazardous Wastes
- **Category:** `Short Answer & Definitions`
- **Marks / Importance:** 1M | CRITICAL (⭐⭐⭐⭐⭐)
- **Exam Appearances:** EST End Exam 2024 (Section II, Q(a)), End Exam 2023 (Section I, Q1(e)), End Exam 2022 (Section III, Q12)

#### Expected Answer
**Exam Calibrated Answer: 1 Mark**

**The Basel Convention (1989 / 1992):**

An international treaty under UNEP designed to reduce the transboundary movements of hazardous wastes, specifically to **prohibit toxic colonialism**—the dumping of hazardous electronic waste and chemical refuse from developed nations into developing countries without prior informed consent and sound environmental management facilities.

#### Key Points
- **Treaty Identity:** The **Basel Convention on the Control of Transboundary Movements of Hazardous Wastes and Their Disposal** is a comprehensive multilateral environmental agreement adopted in 1989 (entered into force in 1992).

- **Core Regulatory Objective:** Engineered specifically to regulate and strictly restrict the transboundary movement of hazardous chemical and electronic wastes, preventing industrialized, wealthy nations from dumping toxic waste on developing countries that lack environmentally sound recycling technologies.

- **Operational Framework:** Requires Prior Informed Consent (PIC) before any cross-border transfer of hazardous waste can legally proceed, and categorizes unauthorized waste transfers as illegal trafficking.

- **Relevance to Developing Nations:** Addresses the widespread illegal shipment of e-waste from Europe, the USA, and Japan into informal processing hubs in Asia and Africa (e.g. Guiyu in China and Agbogbloshie in Ghana), where unprotected workers and local food chains suffer catastrophic toxic contamination.

- **Indian Statutory Alignment:** Governs India's import restrictions; under India's Foreign Trade Policy, the import of second-hand computers is heavily restricted and the import of e-waste is strictly prohibited.

#### Detailed Answer
**Context & Historical Genesis:** During the 1980s, tightening environmental regulations and rising waste disposal costs in industrialized nations led to a phenomenon known as "toxic colonialism"—the mass export of hazardous chemical and industrial wastes to developing nations in Africa, Asia, and Latin America, where environmental standards were weak or unenforced. To combat this injustice, the international community negotiated the **Basel Convention on the Control of Transboundary Movements of Hazardous Wastes and Their Disposal** under the auspices of the United Nations Environment Programme (UNEP) in 1989.

#### Core Legal Mandates of the Basel Convention

As documented in Slide 26 under *"Legislations/Framework governing e-waste"*, the Basel Convention serves as the primary international legal instrument for governing the cross-border transit of end-of-life electronics:

  - **Restricting Transboundary Movement:** Hazardous wastes cannot be exported from a member state unless the exporting country lacks the technical capacity and facilities to dispose of the waste in an environmentally sound manner, or the importing country requires the waste as raw materials for certified recycling.

  - **Prior Informed Consent (PIC) Procedure:** Any proposed export of hazardous waste requires formal, written notification to the competent national authority of the importing country, along with explicit, verified consent prior to shipment departure.

  - **Prohibition of Exports to Non-Parties:** Prohibits the transboundary movement of hazardous waste between member states and non-signatory nations.

  - **Criminalization of Illegal Traffic:** Expressly defines illegal shipments of hazardous waste as criminal acts, obligating exporting states to take back intercepted waste at their own expense.

#### Relevance to Global E-Waste Trade & Slide Case Studies

Slides 20, 24, and 26 demonstrate why the Basel Convention is vital to developing countries:

  - *Guiyu, Guangdong, China (Slide 20):* Illustrates how vast amounts of electronic waste were routinely and illegally exported from the USA, Western Europe, and Japan, dumping as much as 4,000 tonnes of toxic e-waste every hour onto roadside scrap operations where unprotected workers dismantled parts with bare hands.

  - *Agbogbloshie, Accra, Ghana (Slide 24):* Highlights a 2019 report showing that electronic waste exported from Europe poisoned local chicken eggs with toxic chlorinated dioxins and PCBs in a community where 80,000 people reside. Exporters repeatedly exploit legal loopholes by falsely mislabeling toxic e-waste shipments as "charitable donations" or "functional second-hand electronics."

  - *India's Trade Policy Integration (Slide 26):* In compliance with Basel principles, India's national Foreign Trade Policy strictly forbids the commercial importation of e-waste and places heavy restrictions on importing second-hand computers to protect the domestic environment from external toxic burdens.

*Slide Reference: Class 14, Slides 20, 24, and 26.*

### [EQB-14-06] What is a Green Website? Give examples and explain how search engine algorithms and display color palettes impact electrical power consumption and carbon emissions.

- **Topic:** Green Website Concept & Examples (Blackle)
- **Category:** `Short Answer & Definitions`
- **Marks / Importance:** 1M – 2M | HIGH (⭐⭐⭐⭐)
- **Exam Appearances:** EST End Exam 2024 (Section II, Q(d) & Section III(d)), End Exam 2022 (Section VI, Q(d))

#### Expected Answer
**Exam Calibrated Answer: 1M – 2M**

**[For 1 Mark — Definition & Mechanism]:**

A **Green Website** is a web portal engineered to minimize energy consumption and carbon emissions across data centers, transmission networks, and end-user displays by employing lightweight code, efficient asset compression, and dark-themed UI that reduces display power draw.

**[If asked for 2 Marks, add Blackle & Pixel Physics (1M)]:**

**Blackle Case Study (Slide 27):** Created as an energy-saving Google search engine based on the premise that CRT, OLED, and plasma displays consume significantly less wattage displaying black pixels (where pixels are dimmed or shut off entirely) than displaying a bright white screen ($15\text{W}$ saving on CRT). Blackle demonstrated savings of over **$10.07\text{ MWh}$ of electrical energy** over its operation.

#### Key Points
- **Definition:** A Green Website is a website engineered, hosted, and operated to minimize electrical energy consumption and carbon emissions across server data centers, telecommunications networks, and client display monitors.

- **Display Energy Dynamics:**
<br>• Traditional CRT and modern OLED displays draw maximum power when illuminating a pure white screen, but draw significantly less electrical power when displaying black or dark pixels.
<br>• CRT monitors consume **80W**, while LCDs consume **35W** and LEDs consume **25W** (Slide 10).

- **Blackle Search Engine Example:** Blackle (an eco-friendly search engine created by Heap Media and powered by Google Custom Search) utilizes a predominantly black background. As documented in Slide 13, as of July 2023, Blackle claims to have saved over **10.07 MWh (Megawatt-hours)** of electrical energy worldwide.

- **Algorithmic Energy & Carbon Footprint:** Algorithm efficiency directly dictates computing cycles and carbon emissions per search query (Slide 11):
<br>• Google Search: $\approx 0.20\text{ g } \text{CO}_2$ per query
<br>• Yahoo Search: $\approx 0.26\text{ g } \text{CO}_2$ per query
<br>• Microsoft Live Search: $\approx 0.32\text{ g } \text{CO}_2$ per query
<br>• AOL Search: $\approx 0.37\text{ g } \text{CO}_2$ per query

- **Core Green Web Best Practices:** Clean lightweight code, dark-mode styling, compressed vector assets, efficient server-side caching, and green web hosting powered by renewable energy.

#### Detailed Answer
**Concept & Definition:** A Green Website is a website designed, developed, and deployed with the specific objective of reducing environmental impact by minimizing the energy consumed during its transmission and rendering. Every web transaction involves a three-part energy chain: (1) backend servers processing queries and database operations, (2) network switches and fiber cables routing packets, and (3) user terminal devices (monitors and processors) rendering the webpage markup. Green web architecture targets efficiencies across all three links.

#### The Blackle Case Study & Display Energy Mechanics

Slides 10, 12, and 13 document the real-world operational concept of **Blackle**. Traditional search engines (like standard Google or Yahoo) present users with an expansive, bright white canvas. On Cathode Ray Tube (CRT) monitors and modern emissive displays (such as OLED and AMOLED screens), rendering a white pixel requires energizing all color subpixels at maximum luminosity:

  - Slide 10 details baseline monitor power demands: a 17-inch CRT monitor consumes **80 Watts**; a 17-inch LCD monitor consumes **35 Watts**; and an LED monitor consumes **25 Watts**.

  - On CRT and OLED screens, displaying pure black turns off electron guns or diode subpixels, cutting display electrical draw by a measurable margin.

  - Based on this display physics principle, Blackle was developed as an energy-saving search interface. Slide 13 provides the official metric: *"As of July 2023, Blackle claims to have saved over **10.07 MWh** of electrical energy."*

#### Algorithmic Efficiency & Search Carbon Footprint

Slide 11 highlights the critical principle that *"The efficiency of algorithms has an impact on the amount of computer resources required for any given computing function."* When millions of users execute billions of daily searches, algorithmic optimization directly impacts global megawatt-hours and greenhouse gas emissions.

Slide 11 quantifies the direct CO2 released per internet search across major platforms:

  
  
    
      | **Google Search** 
      | **$\approx 0.20\text{ grams } \text{CO}_2$** 
      | Most efficient indexed retrieval algorithm (baseline) 
    

    
      | **Yahoo Search** 
      | **$\approx 0.26\text{ grams } \text{CO}_2$** 
      | 30% higher carbon emissions per query than Google 
    

    
      | **Microsoft Live Search** 
      | **$\approx 0.32\text{ grams } \text{CO}_2$** 
      | 60% higher carbon emissions per query than Google 
    

    
      | **AOL Search** 
      | **$\approx 0.37\text{ grams } \text{CO}_2$** 
      | 85% higher carbon emissions per query than Google 
    

  

This empirical comparison proves that refining backend indexing algorithms, reducing redundant database lookups, compressing page payloads, and minimizing unnecessary JavaScript roundtrips directly reduces the carbon footprint of digital services.

*Slide Reference: Class 14, Slides 10, 11, 12, and 13.*

### [EQB-14-07] Explain the environmental impact of manufacturing computers, smartphones, and mobile devices. Detail the raw material footprint, water consumption, fossil fuel requirements, and mineral intensity.

- **Topic:** Environmental Impact of Computer & Smartphone Production (Resource Footprint)
- **Category:** `Descriptive & Case Study`
- **Marks / Importance:** 3M – 5M | HIGH (⭐⭐⭐⭐)
- **Exam Appearances:** EST End Exam 2024 (Section VI, Q1)

#### Expected Answer
**Exam Calibrated Answer: 3M – 5M**

**[For 3 Marks — Three Core Resource Inputs (1M each)]:**

Manufacturing a single desktop computer and 17-inch monitor ($\sim 24\text{ kg}$) requires at least **1.8 tons of raw physical resources** (Slide 12):

- **Fossil Fuels (1 Mark):** $\sim 240\text{ kg}$ of fossil fuels—nearly **10 times the computer's own physical weight**—expended in ultra-high-temperature semiconductor fabrication.

- **Chemical Reagents (1 Mark):** $\sim 22\text{ kg}$ of industrial specialty chemicals, corrosive acids, and high-purity gases (hydrofluoric acid, silane, toxic dopants).

- **Water Footprint (1 Mark):** $\sim 1,500\text{ kg (1,500 liters)}$ of freshwater, primarily converted to Ultra-Pure Water (UPW) to rinse microchips between lithographic layers.

**[If asked for 5 Marks, add]:**

<ol start="4">
- **Trace Mineral Intensity (1 Mark):** Over **35 different critical elements and minerals** (gold, tantalum, coltan, rare earths), requiring several tons of raw mineral ore extraction per machine.

- **Lifecycle Carbon Imbalance (1 Mark):** Over **$70\text{--}80\%$ of a computer's total lifetime carbon footprint** is embedded upfront in the manufacturing stage, making extending product lifespan the most effective green strategy.

</ol>

#### Key Points
- **The 1.8-Ton Material Footprint:** Constructing a single average **24-kg desktop computer and 27-cm monitor** requires at least:
<br>• **240 kg of fossil fuel**
<br>• **22 kg of chemicals**
<br>• **1,500 kg of water**
<br>• **Totaling 1.8 tons of physical resources**—equivalent to the weight of a full-size sports utility vehicle (SUV)!

- **Extreme Material Intensity Ratio:** Computer manufacturing is vastly more materials-intensive than heavy manufacturing (automobiles or refrigerators). An automobile or refrigerator requires only **1 to 2 times its own weight in fossil fuels**, whereas a computer requires **10 times its own weight in fossil fuels** (240 kg fossil fuels for a 24 kg computer).

- **Semiconductor Water Depletion:** Semiconductor fabrication plants consume astronomical volumes of ultra-pure process water—often equivalent to the water consumption of an entire small city.

- **Smartphone Mineral Intensity:** Fabricating a modern smartphone requires around **35 different raw materials**. After silicon, plastic is the second most-used material. Devices contain finished fractions of iron, aluminium, copper, lead, zinc, tin, nickel, and barium.

- **Mining & Extraction Destruction:** Mineral extraction is responsible for widespread deforestation, landscape degradation, water pollution, and massive atmospheric emissions of CO and CO2 from heavy mining machinery and transport.

- **Embodied Lifecycle Energy & Waste:** Manufacturing a desktop PC and 17" monitor consumes **3,244 MJ of energy**, **920 liters of process water**, and emits **193 kg of greenhouse gases**, generating **37 kg of non-hazardous waste** and **0.7 kg of hazardous waste** over its lifecycle.

#### Detailed Answer
**The Hidden Upstream Footprint:** Popular perception often assumes that the primary environmental impact of electronic gadgets occurs during their daily operation (electricity drawn from wall sockets). However, the lecture slides reveal that the overwhelming majority of technological environmental damage occurs upstream during **raw material extraction, chemical processing, and cleanroom fabrication**.

#### The 1.8-Ton Resource Equivalence of a Personal Computer

Slide 4 presents a striking quantitative resource breakdown. The construction of a single average **24-kilogram desktop computer and 27-centimeter monitor** demands:

  - At least **240 kg of fossil fuels** (coal, petroleum, natural gas)

  - At least **22 kg of industrial chemicals** (acids, toxic dopants, cleaning solvents)

  - At least **1,500 kg of water**

  - **Totaling 1.8 tons of physical material resources**—which is equal to the weight of a full-size sports utility vehicle (SUV).

#### Comparative Material Intensity: Computers vs Automobiles & Refrigerators

Slide 4 highlights an extraordinary contrast between computing hardware and traditional consumer appliances:

  - Manufacturing an automobile or a household refrigerator requires only **1 to 2 times their finished weight** in fossil fuels.

  - Manufacturing a computer requires **10 times its finished weight in fossil fuels** (240 kg of fossil fuel for a 24 kg machine).

  - This makes microelectronic computing one of the most resource-intensive and environmentally demanding manufacturing processes in human industrial history.

#### Semiconductor Fabrication Water Demand

Slide 4 documents that modern semiconductor cleanrooms use staggering volumes of ultra-pure water (UPW) to rinse microchip wafers between lithographic etching and chemical vapor deposition cycles. A single commercial semiconductor manufacturing plant can consume **as much fresh water as an entire small city**, placing severe hydrological stress on municipal reservoirs and local aquifers.

#### Smartphone Mineral Metallurgy & Environmental Degradation

Slide 2 examines the metallurgy of mobile devices:

  
  
    
      | **Mineral Diversity** 
      | Around **35 different raw materials** per smartphone 
      | Demands multi-continent supply chains; large open-pit mining operations cause severe ecological disruption. 
    

    
      | **Core Structural Materials** 
      | Silicon (primary) and **Plastic (second most-used material)** 
      | Silicon refining requires intense electric arc furnace energy; plastics derive from non-renewable petrochemical refining. 
    

    
      | **Metallic Constituents** 
      | Iron, aluminium, copper, lead, zinc, tin, nickel, barium 
      | Smelting and open-pit extraction cause widespread deforestation, topsoil erosion, landscape scarring, and acid mine drainage. 
    

    
      | **Machinery & Atmospheric Burden** 
      | Heavy diesel extraction machinery and smelting furnaces 
      | Releases massive plumes of carbon dioxide (CO2) and carbon monoxide (CO) into ambient air, accelerating global climate change. 
    

  

#### Embodied Energy & Lifecycle Waste Matrix

Slide 15 provides further lifecycle quantification: producing a standard PC and 17" monitor consumes **3,244 Mega Joules (MJ) of energy**, **920 liters of process water**, and emits **193 kg of greenhouse gases**. Throughout its lifetime, this single computer system generates **37 kg of non-hazardous solid waste** and **0.7 kg of hazardous chemical waste**. Because these environmental costs are locked in before the consumer ever powers on the device, extending device lifespans is paramount to sustainable computing.

*Slide Reference: Class 14, Slides 2, 4, and 15.*

### [EQB-14-08] How can computers be made green? Explain key operational measures, paper conservation metrics, and describe at least five sustainable concept computers featured in green computing design.

- **Topic:** Sustainable Concept Computers & Design Innovations for Green Computing
- **Category:** `Comprehensive Descriptive`
- **Marks / Importance:** 5M – 10M | CRITICAL (⭐⭐⭐⭐⭐)
- **Exam Appearances:** End Exam Curriculum (Class 14 Core Syllabus)

#### Expected Answer
**Exam Calibrated Answer: 5M – 10M**

**1. Office Paper Mathematics (Slide 13) [2 Marks]:**

- **The 24-Tree Rule:** Producing 1 ton of virgin office paper consumes **24 living mature trees**.

- **The Ream Calculation:** 1 tree yields $\approx 16.67$ reams of paper (8,333 sheets). Consuming 1 ream of paper (500 sheets) consumes approximately **$6\%$ of a living tree**.

- **Recycling Benefit:** Recycling 1 ton of paper saves 17 mature trees, $7,000\text{ gallons}$ of water, and 4,000 kWh of electricity.

**2. Operational IT Greening Protocols [3 Marks]:**

- **Print Management (1M):** Enforcing default duplex (double-sided) printing, digital PDF workflows, and draft-mode toner settings.

- **Display & PC Power Management (1M):** Automated aggressive sleep timers (10-minute idle display shutdown, 20-minute system sleep), saving up to 70% of desktop electricity.

- **Procurement Standard (1M):** Purchasing only EPEAT Gold and Energy Star compliant modular hardware that allows independent CPU/RAM upgrades.

**[If asked for 10 Marks, add Concept Green Computers (Slide 24) [5 Marks]]:**

- *Nikola Knezevic Solar Concept:* Fold-out solar panel charging internal battery in transit.

- *Lawn PC:* Photovoltaic grass-blade lawn chassis generating zero-carbon compute power.

- *Dell 'O' Biodegradable PC:* 100% biodegradable bamboo/cornstarch casing with seed-embedded casing.

- *Igglu Modular PC:* Hot-swappable modular functional blocks eliminating disposal of entire machine during component upgrades.

#### Key Points
- **Operational & Behavioral Measures:**
<br>• Turn off computers and printers when not in use; set systems to sleep modes when idle.
<br>• Prefer energy-efficient laptops over power-hungry desktops.
<br>• Select low-power displays: upgrade from CRT monitors (80W) to LCD (35W) or LED (25W).
<br>• Deploy Energy Star rated computers (achieving 80% energy efficiency vs 70% for unrated systems).

- **Paper Consumption Mathematics (Slide 18):**
<br>• **1 ton of office paper** consumes **24 trees**.
<br>• **1 tree** yields **16.67 reams of paper** (with 1 ream using **6% of a tree**).
<br>• **1 ton of recycled paper** saves **17 trees**.

- **Featured Sustainable Concept Computers (Slides 29–35):**
<br>1. *Nikola Knezevic Solar Laptop:* Features a fold-out solar panel on the top half that charges battery in both open and closed positions.
<br>2. *Lawn PC:* Powered by recyclable solar grass blades with integrated circuits and solar cells, saving **876 kWh of power per year**.
<br>3. *Dell O Project & Biodegradable PC:* Employs laser floor projection to eliminate physical monitors and keyboards; skin made from bio-decomposable starch-based polymer, bamboo, and cornstarch.
<br>4. *Igglu Modular PC:* Uses standard reusable and recyclable modular components (PCI drives, graphics cards, RAM, HDDs) for simple, zero-waste upgrades.
<br>5. *Power Flower PC:* Powered by a hydrogen fuel cell; dripping water is collected in a clear container and reused for drinking or cooking; gesture camera eliminates mouse/keyboard; projector replaces monitor.
<br>6. *Solar-Powered Clamshell PC for Rural India:* Mini PC with roll-out LCD screen, built-in LED projector, and solar panels to provide agricultural advisory to farmers.

#### Detailed Answer
**Actionable Strategies for Green Computing:** Slide 18 poses the core practical question: *"How can I make my computer Green?"* The slide outlines a systematic methodology: developing an institutional green computing plan, making environmentally sound purchasing decisions, conserving electrical energy, recycling obsolete hardware, and drastically reducing office paper consumption.

#### The Mathematics of Office Paper Conservation

Slide 18 details the exact ecological metrics connecting digital computing to forestry conservation:

  - **1 ton of office paper** requires the destruction of **24 mature trees**.

  - **1 tree** yields approximately **16.67 reams of paper** (meaning 1 single ream of 500 sheets consumes **6% of a tree**).

  - Manufacturing and using **1 ton of recycled paper** preserves **17 mature trees** from commercial clear-cutting.

#### Operational Energy Conservation Standards

Slide 10 establishes fundamental hardware and operational practices:

  - **Hardware Form-Factor:** Laptops must be preferred over desktop PCs due to their compact, low-voltage mobile chipsets and passive cooling.

  - **Display Selection:** Transitioning away from 17-inch CRT monitors (**80 Watts**) to 17-inch LCD panels (**35 Watts**) or LED monitors (**25 Watts**) slashes display energy consumption by up to 68%.

  - **Energy Star Compliance:** Energy Star rated computing devices operate at **80% electrical efficiency**, compared to only **70% efficiency** for non-rated computers.

  - **Active Power Management:** Enforcing automated sleep states and shutting down machines and printers when not in use stops idle CPU and cooling fan energy waste (recognizing that screen savers consume active power).

#### Comprehensive Review of Sustainable Concept Computers

Slides 29 through 35 present twelve pioneering industrial design concepts that reimagine computing hardware for circularity and sustainability:

  
  
    
      | **Nikola Knezevic Solar Laptop** 
      | Slide 30 
      | Fold-out integrated solar harvesting 
      | Features a solar panel attached to its top half that folds out to catch sunlight whether the laptop is open or closed, maintaining a topped-up battery pack and saving grid electricity. 
    

    
      | **Lawn PC** 
      | Slide 30 
      | Self-generating biomimetic solar cells 
      | Engineered with recyclable synthetic grass blades embedding micro-circuits and solar cells; generates its own power and saves approximately **876 kWh of electrical power per year**. 
    

    
      | **Dell O Project** 
      | Slide 29 
      | Peripheral-free floor projection 
      | Uses high-end laser projection to project the display and virtual keyboard onto the floor, eliminating physical monitors and keyboards. Body carved from bio-decomposable starch-based polymer; obsolete parts returnable to manufacturer. 
    

    
      | **Dell Biodegradable Desktop** 
      | Slide 29 
      | Agricultural polymer casing 
      | Chassis skin composed of biodegradable polymers synthesized from bamboo and cornstarch. Components can be returned to Dell for certified recycling upon obsolescence. 
    

    
      | **Igglu Modular PC** 
      | Slide 31 
      | Standardized modular upgradeability 
      | Built from standardized, reusable, and recyclable modular blocks for PCI drives, graphics cards, RAM, and hard drives, allowing users to upgrade individual components without discarding the chassis. 
    

    
      | **Compaq EOS Sustainable Desktop**

      | Engineered for rapid disassembly and partial recycling with a tiny desk footprint; utilizes biodegradable bio-resins and recyclable aluminum; supported by HP's hardware take-back program.

### [EQB-14-09] Explain the regulatory framework and legislations governing e-waste management. Why is dedicated e-waste legislation necessary, and what are the salient features of the Central Pollution Control Board (CPCB) 2008 Guidelines?

- **Topic:** E-Waste Legislations & Regulatory Guidelines (EPR, RoHS, CPCB 2008 Guidelines)
- **Category:** `Descriptive & Regulatory Frameworks`
- **Marks / Importance:** 3M – 5M | CRITICAL (⭐⭐⭐⭐⭐)
- **Exam Appearances:** End Exam Curriculum (Class 14 Regulatory Framework)

#### Expected Answer
**Exam Calibrated Answer: 3M – 5M**

**[For 3 Marks — Three Statutory Reasons for Dedicated E-Waste Laws (1M each)]:**

- **Unique Post-Consumer Nature (1 Mark):** Unlike continuous point-source industrial waste, e-waste originates from millions of dispersed post-consumer households and offices, making conventional factory pollution laws inapplicable.

- **Complex Hazardous-Precious Material Matrix (1 Mark):** Electronics contain both highly toxic heavy metals ($\text{Pb, Cd, Hg}$) and valuable precious metals ($\text{Au, Ag, Cu, Pd}$), creating economic incentives for dangerous informal backyard recycling if unregulated.

- **Extended Producer Responsibility (EPR) Mandate (1 Mark):** Mandates that electronics manufacturers and brand owners finance and organize take-back buyback schemes, establishing formal collection targets and RoHS substance bans.

**[If asked for 5 Marks, add CPCB 2008 Guidelines Framework (2 Marks)]:**

- **CPCB Guidelines (2008):** Laid down the formal definition of e-waste, identified toxic constituent risks, established environmentally sound dismantling/recycling standards, and paved the way for India's dedicated **E-Waste (Management and Handling) Rules, 2011**.

#### Key Points
- **Why Dedicated E-Waste Legislation is Necessary (Slide 25):**
<br>• E-waste is **distinct** because it is an *end-of-consumption waste*, whereas hazardous waste typically results from a distinct industrial manufacturing process.
<br>• The e-waste value chain is exceptionally complex, involving diverse stakeholders: manufacturers/producers, distributors, retailers, end consumers, collection agencies, and informal/formal recyclers.
<br>• E-waste management involves refurbishment for reuse, physical dismantling, and precious metal recovery—processes requiring specialized technical standards.
<br>• Precedents under the Environment Protection Act exist for waste with distinct characteristics (e.g. Biomedical Wastes Rules 1998, Batteries Rules 2001).

- **Multi-Pillar Legal Framework Governing E-Waste (Slide 26):**
<br>• *Hazardous Waste Rules 1998 (as amended in 2008):* Governs toxic contents; establishes mandatory registration for e-waste recyclers.
<br>• *Municipal Solid Waste Management & Handling Rules:* Covers non-toxic solid waste fractions.
<br>• *Basel Convention:* Regulates transboundary movement to prevent international toxic dumping.
<br>• *Foreign Trade Policy:* Restricts the importation of second-hand computers and strictly prohibits the import of e-waste into India.

- **Salient Features of CPCB Guidelines (April 2008) (Slide 27):**
<br>1. **Producer Responsibility (Extended Producer Responsibility / EPR):** Establishes that manufacturers are responsible for the entire lifecycle, including financing and organizing take-back and recycling systems.
<br>2. **RoHS (Restriction on Hazardous Substances):** Limits hazardous heavy metals (lead, cadmium, mercury, hexavalent chromium) and flame retardants in electronic goods.
<br>3. **Best Practices & Recycling Technology:** Provides technical insights and standards for environmentally sound recycling across dismantling, precious metal recovery, and hazardous disposal.
<br>4. Explicitly stated the legal necessity for a dedicated standalone statute to enforce Producer Responsibility.

#### Detailed Answer
**The Legal Dilemma of E-Waste:** Historically, discarded electronics in India were lumped under general hazardous industrial waste or municipal solid waste regulations. However, the lecture slides detail why conventional environmental statutes failed to manage electronic scrap effectively.

#### The Need for Dedicated / Separate E-Waste Legislation

Slide 25 articulates four definitive legal and operational reasons why e-waste requires dedicated, standalone statutes:

  - **End-of-Consumption Nature:** E-waste is legally and physically distinct from traditional industrial hazardous waste. Industrial waste originates from point-source manufacturing processes inside factories, whereas e-waste is generated at the end of consumer utilization across millions of distributed households, schools, and offices.

  - **Statutory Precedents under the Environment Protection Act:** The Indian Environment Protection Act provides for separate, specialized rules for waste streams possessing unique lifecycle characteristics. Precedents include the *Biomedical Wastes (Management & Handling) Rules 1998* and the *Batteries (Management & Handling) Rules 2001*. E-waste demanded an equivalent specialized statutory regime.

  - **Complex Multi-Stakeholder Value Chain:** Unlike point-source factory emissions, the e-waste lifecycle spans an intricate network of participants: original equipment manufacturers (producers), brand distributors, retail vendors, millions of corporate and individual end consumers, waste pickers, scrap aggregators, and formal/informal dismantlers.

  - **Dual Characteristics (Valuable Resource vs Toxic Hazard):** E-waste is unique because it combines high economic value (gold, silver, copper) with acute environmental hazards (lead, mercury, cadmium). Proper handling requires specialized refurbishment for reuse, safe dismantling, and hydrometallurgical/pyrometallurgical precious metal reclamation.

#### The Multi-Tiered Legal Framework Governing E-Waste

Slide 26 outlines the historical intersection of statutes governing e-waste in India prior to dedicated standalone rules:

  
  
    
      | **Hazardous Waste (M&H) Rules 1998 (Amended 2008)** 
      | Toxic chemical constituents 
      | Made formal environmental registration **mandatory for all electronic recyclers**. 
    

    
      | **Municipal Solid Waste (M&H) Rules** 
      | Non-toxic waste fractions 
      | Governs mixed municipal solid waste collection, sorting, and landfill disposal. 
    

    
      | **Basel Convention** 
      | International transboundary movements 
      | Prohibits transboundary shipment of toxic e-waste to non-equipped developing states. 
    

    
      | **Foreign Trade Policy (DGFT)** 
      | Cross-border commercial trade 
      | Restricts import of second-hand PCs and **strictly prohibits import of e-waste**. 
    

    
      | **CPCB Guidelines (April 2008)** 
      | National environmental guidance 
      | Established fundamental principles: Producer Responsibility, RoHS, and recycling standards. 
    

  

#### Salient Features of CPCB Guidelines 2008

Slide 27 details the landmark guidelines notified by the Central Pollution Control Board (CPCB) in April 2008. These served as India's primary transitional blueprint, articulating four cornerstone principles:

  - **Extended Producer Responsibility (EPR):** Formally recognized the principle that producers and brand owners bear financial and physical responsibility for establishing take-back mechanisms, collection centers, and certified recycling infrastructure for their post-consumer products.

  - **Restriction on Hazardous Substances (RoHS):** Mandated thresholds restricting hazardous substances (lead, cadmium, mercury, hexavalent chromium, polybrominated biphenyls, and polybrominated diphenyl ethers) in newly manufactured electronic products.

  - **Best Practices & Recycling Technology:** Provided engineering blueprints and environmental safeguards for dismantling, mechanical shredding, hydrometallurgical recovery, and toxic fraction disposal.

  - **Call for Dedicated Legislation:** Crucially, the guidelines explicitly documented that non-binding guidelines were insufficient, formally recommending the enactment of standalone, enforceable e-waste legislation—which materialized as the landmark E-Waste (Management and Handling) Rules.

*Slide Reference: Class 14, Slides 25, 26, and 27.*

### [EQB-14-10] Discuss the environmental and occupational health hazards associated with informal e-waste recycling. Detail the case studies of Guiyu (China) and Agbogbloshie (Ghana) as highlighted in the lecture slides.

- **Topic:** Informal E-Waste Recycling Hazards & Case Studies (Guiyu & Agbogbloshie)
- **Category:** `Descriptive & Case Study`
- **Marks / Importance:** 3M – 5M | CRITICAL (⭐⭐⭐⭐⭐)
- **Exam Appearances:** End Exam Past Papers (Class 14 Case Studies)

#### Expected Answer
**Exam Calibrated Answer: 3M – 5M**

**[For 3 Marks — Three Critical Hazards of Informal E-Waste Recycling (1M each)]:**

- **Uncontrolled Toxic Exposure & Methods (1 Mark):** Crude informal methods—open-pit cable burning to extract copper, and acid baths (aqua regia) on hot plates to extract gold—release neurotoxic lead fumes, brominated flame retardants, and dioxins directly into breathing zones.

- **Severe Food Chain Contamination (1 Mark):** Heavy metals and persistent organic pollutants bioaccumulate in surrounding soils and water bodies. (e.g. 2019 Basel Action Network report in Agbogbloshie, Ghana: free-range chicken eggs contained chlorinated dioxins at **220 times European Food Safety Authority limits**).

- **Vulnerable Workforce Exploitation (1 Mark):** Over 90% of informal recycling in developing nations is conducted by women and children without personal protective equipment (PPE), causing chronic neurological deficits, silicosis, and kidney failure.

**[If asked for 5 Marks, add Guiyu Case Study (2 Marks)]:**

- **Guiyu, China Case Study (Slide 22):** Processed up to 4,000 tonnes of e-waste per hour; clinical testing revealed over **$80\%$ of local school children suffered from severe clinical lead poisoning** ($\text{blood lead levels} > 10\,\mu\text{g/dL}$), and local Lianjiang river water was completely undrinkable.

#### Key Points
- **Informal Recycling Reality in India & Developing Nations (Slide 22):**
<br>• Concentrated heavily in the informal (unorganized) sector without organized collection systems.
<br>• Operations are mostly illegal, unregulated, and employ highly polluting rudimentary methods.
<br>• Activities focus on manual dismantling, sale of usable parts, crude resource recovery (acid leaching, open wire burning), and exporting residues for overseas smelting.

- **Key Hazards & Concerns of Informal Recycling (Slide 23):**
<br>• *High-Risk Backyard Operations:* Workers operate in residential alleys with no personal protective equipment (PPE) or toxic ventilation.
<br>• *Non-Sound Technologies:* Acid leaching and open-air burning suffer from extremely low resource recovery efficiencies, causing vast losses of precious materials.
<br>• *Disproportionate Social Impact:* Severely harms vulnerable groups—especially **women, young children, and migrant labourers** who manually sort and desolder circuit boards.

- **Guiyu, Guangdong, China Case Study (Slide 20):**
<br>• World's largest informal e-waste dumping hub, where piles of electronic scrap line roadsides.
<br>• As much as **4,000 tonnes of toxic e-waste** discarded every hour, with massive illegal shipments from Europe, the USA, and Japan processed by unprotected workers.

- **Agbogbloshie, Accra, Ghana Case Study (Slide 24):**
<br>• *"Rotten eggs: e-waste from Europe poisons Ghana's food chain"* (The Guardian, 2019).
<br>• Burning discarded European computers and refrigerators in a scrap settlement of 80,000 people liberated heavy metals, dioxins, and PCBs into soils.
<br>• Foraging free-range chickens ingested contaminated ash; eggs were found to contain lethal concentrations of chlorinated dioxins and PCBs, directly poisoning the human food supply.

#### Detailed Answer
**The Reality of Informal E-Waste Recycling:** While electronic scrap holds immense economic value due to embedded precious metals (gold, silver, platinum, copper), over 90% of recycling in developing nations takes place within the unorganized, informal sector. The lecture slides examine the profound human and ecological crises generated by informal recycling practices.

#### The Recycling Scenario in India & Developing Economies

Slide 22 describes the baseline reality of domestic e-waste processing:

  - E-waste recycling is overwhelmingly concentrated in the informal, unorganized sector.

  - No systematic, organized municipal collection system prevails; waste pickers (kabadiwalas) acquire scrap directly from consumers and offices.

  - Backyard operations are largely illegal and exempt from environmental inspections.

  - Processing methods are primitive and highly polluting: manual smashing of cathode ray tubes, open-pan acid leaching using aqua regia to strip gold from printed circuit boards, and open burning of insulated wiring over burning vehicle tires to harvest copper.

  - Recycling operations encompass: manual dismantling, sale of salvaged components, crude resource recovery, and export of processed electronic scrap for overseas precious metal refining.

#### Occupational & Environmental Hazards

Slide 23 identifies five critical concerns associated with informal processing:

  - **High-Risk Backyard Operations:** Carried out in dense residential settlements with zero effluent treatment, fume hoods, or safety equipment.

  - **Inefficient & Non-Sound Technologies:** Crude open chemical leaching achieves recovery rates below 20%, squandering over 80% of valuable mineral resources while discharging gallons of concentrated metal-laden acid directly into municipal gutters.

  - **Severe Occupational Illnesses:** Workers continuously inhale toxic metal fumes (lead, cadmium), hydrochloric acid mists, and brominated flame retardant vapors, causing chronic pulmonary disease, neurological damage, and renal failure.

  - **Exploitation of Vulnerable Groups:** These hazardous backyard operations disproportionately exploit **women, children, and impoverished migrant labourers**, whose dexterous hands are used to desolder electronic components over charcoal burners with no respiratory protection.

#### Case Study 1: Guiyu, Guangdong, China (Slide 20)

Slide 20 highlights Guiyu, historically recognized as the largest informal e-waste hub on the planet. The slide documents:

  - Roadsides lined with massive piles of discarded computer chassis, circuit boards, and broken monitors.

  - An astonishing global throughput: *"As much as 4,000 tonnes of toxic e-waste are discarded every hour."*

  - Vast quantities of electronic scrap were routinely and illegally exported from Europe, the USA, and Japan to Guiyu, where thousands of unprotected laborers broke down parts in open workshops, leaving local rivers heavily saturated with heavy metals and children exhibiting record-high blood lead levels.

#### Case Study 2: Agbogbloshie, Accra, Ghana (Slide 24)

Slide 24 presents a landmark 2019 investigative report published in *The Guardian*: **"Rotten eggs: e-waste from Europe poisons Ghana's food chain."**

  - At the Agbogbloshie dumpsite in Accra, Ghana, an informal settlement of **80,000 residents** lived adjacent to an open burning scrap yard processing old computers, refrigerators, and electronics shipped from Europe.

  - Open-air wire burning generated dense plumes of toxic smoke and deposited ash laden with chlorinated dioxins and polychlorinated biphenyls (PCBs) across surrounding foraging grounds.

  - Free-range chickens foraging on the dumpsite ingested these chemical residues. Subsequent laboratory testing revealed that a single chicken egg contained toxic dioxins and PCBs hundreds of times above European food safety limits, conclusively proving that crude e-waste burning transfers persistent industrial poisons directly into the human food chain.

*Slide Reference: Class 14, Slides 20, 22, 23, and 24.*

### [EQB-14-11] Match the following computer components with their toxic chemical constituents. Explain the technical function of each chemical in the hardware component and its specific environmental and toxicological impact when improperly discarded.

- **Topic:** Computer Components vs Toxic Chemical Constituents
- **Category:** `Match the Following`
- **Marks / Importance:** 5M | CRITICAL (⭐⭐⭐⭐⭐)
- **Exam Appearances:** EST End Exam 2024 (Section I, Q(A)), End Exam 2022 (Section I, Q(A)), End Exam 2018 (Part II, Q3)

#### Expected Answer
**Exam Calibrated Answer: 5 Marks (Slide 21 Component-Toxin Matching)**

**Matching Electronic Components with Toxic Constituents:**

  | **Printed Circuit Boards (PCBs)** 
  | **Lead ($\text{Pb}$) & BFRs** 
  | Neurotoxic damage to central nervous system; endocrine disruption from brominated flame retardants. 

  | **Cathode Ray Tubes (CRTs)** 
  | **Lead ($\text{Pb}$) & Barium ($\text{Ba}$)** 
  | Funnel glass contains 1–4 kg of lead shielding X-rays; bioaccumulative neurotoxin causing intellectual disability. 

  | **SMD Resistors & Semiconductors** 
  | **Cadmium ($\text{Cd}$)** 
  | Chronic inhalation causes irreversible renal kidney damage and osteomalacia (Itai-itai bone disease). 

  | **Flat Screen Backlights (CCFL)** 
  | **Mercury ($\text{Hg}$)** 
  | Elemental mercury vaporizes easily; causes severe tremors, neurological disorders, and bioaccumulates as methylmercury. 

  | **Cables & Computer Housings** 
  | **PVC & Plasticizers** 
  | Open burning of polyvinyl chloride releases highly carcinogenic polychlorinated dioxins and furans.

#### Key Points
- **Authoritative Slide 21 Match:**
<br>1. **Printed circuit boards** $\rightarrow$ **Lead & cadmium**
<br>2. **Cathode ray tubes (CRTs)** $\rightarrow$ **Lead oxide & Cadmium**
<br>3. **Switches & flat screen monitors** $\rightarrow$ **Mercury**
<br>4. **Computer batteries** $\rightarrow$ **Cadmium**
<br>5. **Capacitors and transformers** $\rightarrow$ **Poly Chlorinated Bi-phenyls (PCB)**
<br>6. **Printed circuit boards, plastic casings cable** $\rightarrow$ **Brominated Flame Retardant (BFR)**
<br>7. **Cable insulation/coating** $\rightarrow$ **Poly Vinyl Chloride (PVC)**

- **Toxicological Mechanisms:**
<br>• *Lead:* Neurotoxin damaging central nervous system, brain development, and kidneys.
<br>• *Cadmium:* Bioaccumulative kidney toxin, human carcinogen, and cause of bone demineralization.
<br>• *Mercury:* Volatilizes into vapor; converts to methylmercury, bioaccumulating in aquatic food chains and causing severe neurological damage.
<br>• *PCBs:* Persistent organic pollutants (POPs) acting as potent endocrine disruptors and carcinogens.
<br>• *BFRs:* Release toxic brominated dioxins/furans upon combustion; disrupt endocrine and thyroid functions.
<br>• *PVC:* Chlorinated polymer; open combustion produces highly carcinogenic chlorinated dioxins.

#### Detailed Answer
**Technical Function vs Toxicological Risk:** Modern electronics rely on specific chemical elements and synthetic polymers to achieve high electrical conductivity, dielectric insulation, flame resistance, and structural durability. However, when these devices reach their end-of-life, those exact constituents pose severe environmental and human toxicological hazards if dismantled or landfilled improperly.

#### Authoritative Hardware Component vs Chemical Constituent Match

Slide 21 provides the definitive examination mapping between hardware components and their toxic chemical constituents:

  
  
    
      | 1 
      | **Printed circuit boards** 
      | **Lead & cadmium** 
      | Lead is used in tin-lead solder alloys for electrical interconnections; cadmium is used in SMD chip resistors, infrared detectors, and semiconductor passivators. 
      | Lead is a potent neurotoxin causing cognitive impairment and kidney damage; cadmium causes proximal renal tubular dysfunction and severe bone demineralization (Itai-Itai disease). 
    

    
      | 2 
      | **Cathode ray tubes (CRTs)** 
      | **Lead oxide & Cadmium** 
      | Lead oxide (up to 2–3 kg per monitor) is infused into funnel glass to shield users from X-rays; cadmium is used as a phosphor coating inside the tube face. 
      | When crushed in unlined dumps, lead leaches into acidic groundwater; cadmium dust causes severe respiratory inflammation and pulmonary edema. 
    

    
      | 3 
      | **Switches & flat screen monitors** 
      | **Mercury** 
      | Elemental mercury was historically used in mechanical tilt switches and as an excitation gas inside Cold Cathode Fluorescent Lamps (CCFLs) backlighting older LCD panels. 
      | Easily volatilizes into toxic mercury vapor; settles into aquatic ecosystems where anaerobic bacteria methylate it into bioaccumulative methylmercury, destroying central nervous systems. 
    

    
      | 4 
      | **Computer batteries** 
      | **Cadmium** 
      | Used as the active cathode material in rechargeable Nickel-Cadmium (Ni-Cd) laptop and CMOS backup battery cells. 
      | Extremely persistent and bioaccumulative; classified as a Group 1 human carcinogen by IARC; damages kidneys and calcium metabolism. 
    

    
      | 5 
      | **Capacitors and transformers** 
      | **Poly Chlorinated Bi-phenyls (PCB)** 
      | Historically functioned as dielectric insulating fluids due to exceptional thermal stability and high dielectric constants. 
      | Classified as Persistent Organic Pollutants (POPs) under the Stockholm Convention; immune system suppressor, potent endocrine disruptor, and causes chloracne and liver cancer. 
    

    
      | 6 
      | **Printed circuit boards, plastic casings cable** 
      | **Brominated Flame Retardant (BFR)** 
      | Polybrominated diphenyl ethers (PBDEs) are blended into polymer casings and fiberglass resin boards to retard thermal ignition and satisfy flammability safety ratings. 
      | Bioaccumulates in human adipose tissue and breast milk; disrupts endocrine and thyroid hormone systems; open-air burning generates acutely toxic polybrominated dibenzodioxins and dibenzofurans. 
    

    
      | 7 
      | **Cable insulation / coating** 
      | **Poly Vinyl Chloride (PVC)** 
      | Thermoplastic polymer blended with plasticizers used to coat electrical wiring and power cords for flexible electrical insulation. 
      | Contains chlorine; when burned in open backyard scrapyards to harvest internal copper wire, it pyrolyzes into highly carcinogenic chlorinated dioxins (TCDD) and corrosive hydrochloric acid gas. 
    

  

#### Exam Answering Protocol

When this recurring 5-mark question appears in Section I, candidates must write the exact 7 pairs specified above. To secure full marks, students should provide both the component-constituent match and a one-sentence technical rationale explaining why the element is hazardous upon disposal.

*Slide Reference: Class 14, Slide 21.*
